is bounded in absolute value by $T$. Increasing an integer power of $T$
therefore dominates the function. The same powers remain cofinal in $H$:
if $h$ is algebraic, a monic polynomial for $h$ has finitely many
coefficients, and the usual algebraic root bound
\[
 |h|\le 1+\max_{k<n}|a_k|
 \quad\text{when }h^n+a_{n-1}h^{n-1}+\cdots+a_0=0
\]
is valid in any ordered field. Its right-hand side is bounded by a power
of $T$.

The field $H$ is proper-class-sized because it contains $\No$. Its
complexification $H(i)$ is algebraically closed of characteristic zero.
By \cref{saut:thm:acfhomogeneity}, it is isomorphic as a pure field to $\K$.
Transport $H$ and its conjugation across this isomorphism.
The transported real form has a countable cofinal subset. By contrast,
every set-sized subset of $\No$ is bounded above, so $\No$ has no countable
cofinal subset. A field isomorphism between real closed fields preserves
their unique orders; hence these real forms cannot be isomorphic.
The conjugacy criterion for involutions proves the final assertion.
\end{proof}

This separates two phenomena. The phase family gives distinct embedded
copies of the \emph{same} real form. Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:inequivalentrealform}
gives a different isomorphism type of real form. Neither result supplies a
classification of all real closed class fields whose complexification is
$\K$. \mergetag\ Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:involutions} classifies the real forms
whose involution preserves the valuation ring and $\Oz[i]$
(Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-involutions}); the real form of
Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:inequivalentrealform} is not of that kind, even up to
conjugacy.

\section{Simplicity, the omega-map, and exponential structure}\label{saut:sec:exponential}

The large automorphism groups above forget much of what makes a surreal
number canonical. Adding back suitable structure can impose rigidity, but
different added structures impose different conditions.

\subsection{Simplicity restores the canonical surreal numbers}

Let $<_{s}$ denote proper-prefix simplicity on ordinal-length surreal sign
sequences. A bijection preserving both numerical order and simplicity is
identity. For completeness, fix the empty sign sequence. At a successor
stage, the two immediate simplicity extensions of a fixed sequence lie on
opposite numerical sides, so both are fixed. At a limit stage, the unique
sign sequence whose proper initial segments are the already fixed chain
is fixed. Transfinite induction proves the assertion.

Thus a member of $G_{\No}$ whose real restriction preserves simplicity is
either identity or conjugation; marking $i$ eliminates conjugation. This
is rigidity of an enriched structure, not rigidity of the real closed
field reduct. A transformation such as $S_2$ is allowed to change birthdays
and simplicity and therefore does not contradict it.

\subsection{The omega-map detects exact value preservation}

Suppose $\sigma\in\Aut(\No)$ commutes with $x\mapsto\omega^x$ and fixes
valuation values pointwise. Since $v(\omega^x)=-x$,
\[
 -\sigma(x)=v(\omega^{\sigma(x)})
 =v(\sigma(\omega^x))=v(\omega^x)=-x.
\]
Therefore $\sigma=\id$. The qualifier ``pointwise'' is indispensable:
all surreal field automorphisms preserve the natural valuation \emph{up to}
an ordered group automorphism, and that weaker fact does not give the last
equality. Without pointwise fixation, the displayed computation instead
says that the induced value-group action equals $\sigma$ itself on the
underlying surreal elements. That is a compatibility condition, not a
proof of triviality.

\subsection{Exponential faithfulness: the existing repository result}

The following finite argument is taken from the repository's
\emph{Growth-Scale Rigidity} manuscript \cite{RepoRigidity}. We give the
proof because it is short, powerful, and avoids exchanging an automorphism
with an infinite sum.

\begin{lemma}[Displacement cannot be globally bounded]\label{saut:lem:displacement}
Let $F$ be an ordered field and $\sigma:F\to F$ a nonidentity unital field
endomorphism. Then $\{\sigma(x)-x:x\in F\}$ is cofinal and coinitial in $F$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Write $D(x)=\sigma(x)-x$. The correct multiplication identity is
\begin{equation}\label{saut:eq:displacement}
 D(ab)=\sigma(a)D(b)+bD(a).
\end{equation}
Choose $a$ with $d=D(a)\ne0$. Given $B>0$, put
\[
 b=\frac{2B(1+|\sigma(a)|)}{|d|}.
\]
If both $|D(b)|$ and $|D(ab)|$ were at most $B$, then
\[
 2B(1+|\sigma(a)|)=|bd|
 \le |D(ab)|+|\sigma(a)||D(b)|
 \le B(1+|\sigma(a)|),
\]
a contradiction. Thus absolute displacements have no upper bound.
Since $D(-x)=-D(x)$, both signs occur with arbitrarily large magnitude.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Faithful value-group action]\label{saut:thm:expfaithful}
Let $F$ be an ordered field with an increasing group isomorphism
$E:(F,+)\to(F_{>0},\cdot)$, and let $w:F^\times\to\Lambda$ be a nontrivial
convex valuation. If an automorphism $\sigma$ commutes with $E$ and fixes
all $w$-values, then $\sigma=\id$.
Hence the exponential automorphisms stabilizing the valuation ring act
faithfully on its value group.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The homomorphism
\[
 \nu:F\to\Lambda,\qquad \nu(x)=w(E(x))
\]
is additive, with image exactly $w(F^\times)$: for $a\ne0$ use
$x=E^{-1}(|a|)$ and $w(-1)=0$ to get $\nu(x)=w(a)$.
Thus it is onto the actual value group, even if $\Lambda$ was presented
as a larger ambient group. Nontriviality of $w$ makes
its kernel $H$ a proper convex additive
subgroup: $E$ is increasing and a convex valuation reverses the order on
positive elements. A proper convex additive subgroup is bounded above in
$F$; if $b>0$ lies outside it, then every $h\in H$ satisfies $|h|<b$.

Commutation and pointwise value fixation give, for every $x$,
\[
 \nu(\sigma x-x)
 =w(E(\sigma x))-w(E(x))
 =w(\sigma(E(x)))-w(E(x))=0.
\]
Thus all displacements lie in the bounded subgroup $H$.
Lemma~\ref{saut:lem:displacement} forces $\sigma=\id$.
Taking the kernel of the induced-action homomorphism proves faithfulness.
\end{proof}

For the usual surreal exponential, the kernel $H$ is exactly the finite
real-surreal elements. Indeed, bounded $x$ gives bounded $\exp x$ and
bounded reciprocal by comparison with $e^n$; conversely, if $\exp x$ and
its reciprocal are bounded, comparison with integers and application of
$\log$ bound $x$. Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{saut:eq:expfaithful}
 \ker\bigl(\Gexp\longrightarrow\Gh\bigr)=\{\id\}.
\end{equation}
In particular, a nonidentity surreal $1$-automorphism cannot preserve the
total real exponential. This settles the precise mathematical alternative
posed in Question~5.4 of the inspected arXiv version of \cite{KKS}, by the
repository proof above; it is not a new result of this article.
Faithfulness does not say that the exponential automorphism group is
trivial. It says that no nonidentity member can hide all its motion below
the valuation scale.

\subsection{A simple obstruction to exponential lifting}

An ordered automorphism of the value group always has the Hahn lift
$M_{\id,\tau}$. It need not have \emph{any} lift preserving the total
surreal exponential. One elementary instance, also covered in
\cite{RepoRigidity}, is worth spelling out.

\begin{proposition}\label{saut:prop:rationalnonlift}
For $q\in\Q_{>0}$ with $q\ne1$, the value-group dilation $g\mapsto qg$
is not induced by any exponential automorphism of $\No$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
If $\sigma$ were such an automorphism, the homomorphism
$\nu(x)=v(\exp x)$ would satisfy
$\nu(\sigma x)=q\nu(x)=\nu(qx)$, using the $\Q$-linearity of the additive
map $\nu$. Thus
$\sigma(x)-qx$ would be finite for every $x$.
Choose a positive infinite $X$, and write
\[
 \sigma(X)=qX+e,\qquad \sigma(X^2)=qX^2+f
\]
with $e,f$ finite. Multiplicativity yields
\[
 q^2-q+\frac{2qe}{X}+\frac{e^2-f}{X^2}=0.
\]
The last two terms are infinitesimal. Taking standard part gives
$q^2-q=0$, contradicting $q>0$, $q\ne1$.
\end{proof}

Thus even the very simple field automorphism $S_2$ cannot be repaired by
composing with a value-fixing automorphism to obtain an exponential lift
of its value action.

\subsection{A global surcomplex operation that remembers real exponential}

There is no need to postulate a total complex logarithm. The operation
\begin{equation}\label{saut:eq:L}
 L:\K^\times\to\No\subset\K,
 \qquad L(z)=\log|z|
\end{equation}
is already globally defined, using only the usual real surreal logarithm.
Say that $\alpha$ preserves $L$ when
$\alpha(L(z))=L(\alpha(z))$ for every nonzero $z$.

\begin{theorem}[Logarithmic-modulus automorphisms]\label{saut:thm:L}
For $\K=\No(i)$,
\begin{equation}\label{saut:eq:LAUT}
 \AutL(\K)\simeq\Gexp\times C_2.
\end{equation}
Its action on the natural value group has kernel exactly $\{\id,c\}$.
Marking $i$ makes that action faithful.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The range of $L$ is all of $\No$, because $L(\exp x)=x$. A commuting
automorphism and its inverse therefore preserve $\No$. Write it as
$\widehat\sigma c^e$. For $r>0$ real-surreal,
\[
 \sigma(\log r)=\alpha(L(r))=L(\sigma r)=\log(\sigma r).
\]
Thus $\sigma$ commutes with logarithm and hence with exponential.
Conversely, if $\sigma$ is exponential, the norm equivariance
\eqref{saut:eq:equivariantnorm} proves that both lifts commute with $L$.
The kernel statement follows from \cref{saut:thm:expfaithful} and the fact that
conjugation fixes every valuation value.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:L} is another result already present in
\cite{RepoRigidity}. Notice that $L$ is required to transform
\emph{equivariantly}: $L(\alpha z)=\alpha(Lz)$. Demanding instead
$L(\alpha z)=Lz$ pointwise would impose a stronger condition.

\subsection{Local exponentials and derivations are different structures}

For $\varepsilon\in\mm$, the Hahn series
\[
 \operatorname{Exp}(\varepsilon)=\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{\varepsilon^n}{n!},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Log}(1+\varepsilon)
   =\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{n+1}\varepsilon^n}{n}
\]
are well-defined by positive-support summability. Any strongly
$\C$-linear element of $G_v$ commutes with these operations, simply by
applying strong summation and the field laws. This includes the phase
twists, the exponent dilations, and the constant-fixing flows above.
For finite $z=a+\varepsilon$, such an automorphism also respects the
finite exponential $e^a\operatorname{Exp}(\varepsilon)$.
None of this implies compatibility with the total real surreal exponential
on infinite arguments, as $S_2$ and \cref{saut:prop:rationalnonlift} demonstrate.

The Berarducci--Mantova construction equips $\No$ with a nonzero derivation
compatible with substantial exponential and transseries structure
\cite{BM}. Fix one such derivation $\partial$ and extend it to $\K$ by
\[
 \partial(x+iy)=\partial x+i\partial y.
\]
For real-axis-preserving automorphisms, a direct coordinate calculation gives
\[
 \{\alpha\in G_{\No}:\alpha\partial=\partial\alpha\}
 \simeq\Aut(\No,\partial)\times C_2.
\]
This is a reduction, not a classification of the differential group or of
all differential automorphisms without a marked real axis. In particular,
the scalar derivation $\partial$, formal differentiation of a power series,
and the fine derivative of a function are three distinct notions. The
zero-derivative automorphism in \cref{saut:thm:zeroderiv} concerns the third.

\section{Consequences for algebra, geometry, and symbolic computation}\label{saut:sec:applications}

Automorphisms transport identities, but a transported identity must include
all its coefficients and all its chosen structures. This elementary rule is
especially useful when applying surcomplex theory.

\subsection{Polynomial and finite-dimensional invariants}

If $p(X)=\sum_{k=0}^n a_kX^k$ and $\alpha$ is a field automorphism, put
$p^\alpha(X)=\sum_{k=0}^n\alpha(a_k)X^k$. Then
\[
 \alpha(p(z))=p^\alpha(\alpha z).
\]
Roots, root multiplicities, common-root relations, resultants,
discriminants, and finite matrix determinants therefore transform
algebraically. When all coefficients are fixed, the root multiset is
permuted. Without coefficient fixation, it is the roots of the
\emph{transformed} polynomial that are obtained.

For $\alpha\in G_v$, root separations satisfy
\[
 v(\alpha r_j-\alpha r_k)=\tau_\alpha(v(r_j-r_k)).
\]
Thus finite valuation-cluster trees retain their combinatorial incidence,
although the labels can be reindexed. For $\alpha\in\U$, every nonzero
separation even retains its leading term. These statements follow from
additivity and the valuation laws; they require no compactness or ordinary
analytic convergence.

For $\alpha\in G_{\No}$, Hermitian algebra is also transported, because
$\alpha$ commutes with conjugation. The identity
$A^*=\overline A^{\,T}$ transforms to $(\alpha A)^*=\alpha(A^*)$,
and positive real scalars remain positive. Under a phase twist not
preserving the axis, the same conclusion holds only after conjugation is
also transported to $j_\theta$. Keeping the original conjugation while
changing the scalars is not an invariant operation.

\subsection{Automorphism tests as checks on a proposed canonical definition}

Suppose a construction is claimed to depend only on the pure field
$(\K,+,\cdot)$ and on a specified set of scalar parameters. It must commute
with every automorphism fixing those parameters. Proposition~\ref{saut:prop:nondefinability}
then immediately rules out pure-field definitions of the distinguished
real axis and finite ring. A purported canonical modulus on the bare
field would similarly determine extra real-form information and cannot
be the chosen modulus without additional structure.

This does not make those constructions invalid. It identifies which data
must be declared: conjugation or a real form for Hermitian geometry,
a valuation for scales, monomials and a summation structure for normal-form
algorithms, and an exponential for logarithmic growth. An automorphism
counterexample is often the quickest way to detect an omitted hypothesis.

\subsection{What a symbolic implementation can realistically support}

A symbolic system can implement \eqref{saut:eq:monomial} exactly on a declared
Hahn workspace once it can evaluate the coefficient automorphism, exponent
map, and character, and certify that the exponent map is an ordered
additive bijection. For finite supports, the arithmetic checks reduce to
finite convolution. For symbolic infinite families, well-order and
finite-incidence certificates remain necessary.

The maps $S_a$, $D_\lambda$, and $P_\theta$ are particularly transparent on
normal-form expressions with accessible exponent coefficients. The
fixed-shift flow can be represented by its operator formula or by closed
expressions such as $t\mapsto t/(1-st)$. The coefficient-motion example
requires an abstract derivation on real constants and should not be
advertised as an algorithm on arbitrary numerical real inputs.
Likewise, the back-and-forth existence theorems are not executable
algorithms on unrestricted surcomplex names.

\section{Relation to the Surreal repository and a formalization plan}\label{saut:sec:repo}

This section is the source's comparison, as of its pin. It was checked for
this report and is accurate at the pin; Section~\ref{saut:sec:position}
adds the relations it did not draw, and
Appendix~\ref{saut:app:pinned} records what has changed since.

The inspection in this article uses the repository revision
\begin{center}
\repoSHA.
\end{center}
The root documentation, the report catalogue, the exponential-rigidity
manuscript and its audit, the normal-form bridge note, and the two specific
Lean modules below were read through the GitHub connector. This is a
selected-source inspection, not a claim to have searched every file or to
have rebuilt the project.

The inspected documentation distinguishes research arguments from checked
Lean coverage. In particular, the current bridge note reports an ordered
field equivalence for small normal forms, transfer of real closedness,
actual surcomplex algebraic closedness, and valuation/standard-part
preservation \cite{RepoBridge}. These are \emph{repository-reported}
implementation facts. This article supplies no independent build result
for them.

\subsection{Existing interfaces actually inspected}

\texttt{Surreal/Algebra/Complexify.lean} defines the quadratic algebra
$F[i]$, its imaginary unit, coordinate multiplication, conjugation,
\texttt{normSq}, and the identity \texttt{mul\_conj}. Its field construction
uses an ordered coefficient field and explicitly does not, by itself,
assert algebraic closedness \cite{RepoComplexify}.
These interfaces are sufficient foundations for a generic development of
\cref{saut:thm:axis,saut:thm:norm,saut:thm:circle}.

\texttt{Surreal/Algebra/FineDerivative.lean} defines
\texttt{FineHasDerivAt} by punctured-neighborhood difference quotients and
contains uniqueness, continuity, algebraic differentiation rules, and the
polynomial derivative theorem \cite{RepoFine}. It is an appropriate
interface for \cref{saut:thm:derivative}; the exponent-dilation example additionally
requires a proved normal-form automorphism and its exact valuation formula.

The documentation's statements about smallness must retain their universe
indices. A theorem that every lower-universe subset is closed and discrete
does not say that every subset available in a larger universe is discrete.
The class theory in this article and a universe-relative Lean realization
are compatible approaches, but they are not textually interchangeable.

\subsection{Suggested implementation order}

A useful order is to separate finite algebra from normal-form and global
existence arguments.

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Generic real-form algebra.} Construct the lift of a base-field
ring equivalence to $F[i]$. Prove uniqueness from its restriction and its
value on $i$, and identify the conjugation centralizer. Add exact norm
rigidity and the Cayley-circle criterion. These proofs need no Hahn theory.

\item \textbf{Explicit Hahn automorphisms.} Package coefficient maps,
ordered additive exponent equivalences, and characters in one construction.
Prove the composition law \eqref{saut:eq:monomiallaw} and valuation formula before
introducing the phase action. Keep the nested coefficient extraction
$\ell$ on surreal \emph{exponents} distinct from coefficient extraction
on a surcomplex input.

\item \textbf{Support-certified flows.} Reuse a summable-family interface
and prove the fixed-shift family is jointly summable. Establish inverse
formulas for \cref{saut:thm:coeffflow,saut:thm:shiftflow} through coefficientwise finite
identities. Merely entering a formal expression $\exp(D)$ is not a proof of
an automorphism.

\item \textbf{Topology and derivatives.} Prove the induced homeomorphism
from a valuation reindexing, the nonzero-derivative rigidity theorem for a
generic topological field, and finally $S_a'\equiv0$ for $a>1$ using
\eqref{saut:eq:dilationquot}. No mean-value theorem is needed or available for
that last conclusion.

\item \textbf{Global foundations separately.} Treat class back-and-forth,
real-form existence, and collection-level group factorizations in an
explicit foundational layer or in bounded universes with the appropriate
hypotheses. Do not silently replace a set-stage extension theorem by an
unrestricted type-level statement about every model.
\end{enumerate}

The names in this implementation order describe proposed work; they are
not assertions that corresponding new modules already exist. In particular,
no new Lean code or Lean-checking claim is included in the present package.

\section{What remains to be classified}\label{saut:sec:questions}

The central stabilizer question has a clean answer, and the valued subgroup
has an exact kernel--quotient decomposition. Neither result gives a fully
explicit description of the unrestricted automorphism collection.
The following are useful research targets, with their status kept distinct.

\paragraph{The image of exponential automorphisms on the value group.}
Faithfulness \eqref{saut:eq:expfaithful} determines the kernel, not the image.
An intrinsic necessary-and-sufficient criterion on an ordered additive
$\tau:\No\to\No$ for it to come from an exponential automorphism would
be much stronger. Positive rational dilations already show that arbitrary
Hahn lifting does not solve the problem. Any such criterion must use the
interaction between the value group and real exponential growth, not just
ordered-group structure. (This is Question~13.1, \lbl{q:image}, of the
collection's rigidity report, where it is also open.)

\paragraph{Omega-map compatibility.}
The nontrivial automorphisms preserving the omega-map, and extensions of
order automorphisms of its generalized epsilon fixed points, are posed in
Questions~5.6 and~5.7 of the inspected version of \cite{KKS}. They are not
resolved here. Exact value rigidity only describes one kernel and must not
be mistaken for a solution of those broader extension problems.

\paragraph{Effective descriptions inside the leading-term kernel.}
The abstract support-and-invertibility conditions for strong Hahn
substitutions are meaningful, but finite, compositional certificates for
larger symbolic families would be useful for computation and formalization.
The fixed-shift constructions are a tractable subclass, not an exhaustive
normal form for $\U$ or its strong part. This is a proposed computational
research direction, not a claim that the general Hahn literature lacks
abstract descriptions.

\paragraph{Real-form and continuity classifications.}
The phase family and the cofinality example show that real forms have both
embedding freedom and isomorphism-type freedom. A detailed classification
with additional valuation, summation, or simplicity conditions is a separate
problem. Similarly, this article proves that $G_v$ acts continuously but
does not identify every fine-continuous pure-field automorphism. These are
boundaries of the present account; they are not asserted to be newly
formulated or historically unresolved in every related setting.
\emph{Status (batch~32; \mergetag).} The real-form classification is
\emph{partly answered}. For involutions that preserve the valuation ring and
$\Oz[i]$ (summation is then automatic), Theorems~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-involutions}
and~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-count} give the complete classification: conjugacy types
correspond to real closed fields of transcendence degree $\cc$, exactly $2^{\cc}$ of
them. Real forms whose involution is not both valued and $\Oz[i]$-preserving (for
instance one that is only valued, or only strong), the unrestricted real forms, and
simplicity conditions remain unclassified, as does the continuity question.

\section{Source audit, proof status, and conclusions}\label{saut:sec:audit}

\subsection{Version-sensitive points}

The version-specific literature anchor is Kaplan--Krapp--Serra,
arXiv:2509.22374v3, submitted 23 April 2026, whose PDF is dated 27 April
2026. Its displayed formulas were checked against the PDF, not only the
HTML rendering. Two local cautions affect example construction. The
shift $g-1$ printed in Example~4.3 is not contracting for the convention
$v=\min\supp$; our construction uses a positive shift. The series
$\sum_n\omega^{-1/n}$ printed in Example~4.4 does not have decreasing
Conway support; \eqref{saut:eq:nonstronga} uses a valid support instead.
Our nonstrong example does not claim the stronger global leading-term
property asserted for that source's construction.

The semidirect actions in this article are specified by actual composition
formulas, especially \eqref{saut:eq:monomiallaw} and \eqref{saut:eq:skeletonlaw}.
A display listing factors should not be read as a componentwise direct
product when exponent reindexing acts on coefficient weights.

The repository's exponential-rigidity report is explicitly prior material
at the pinned revision. Its appended source audit records an earlier
catalogue/tree mismatch, illustrating why absence from a catalogue or
failure of code search is not evidence of absence from the repository.
The present article neither relies on that earlier absence claim nor
makes an exhaustive nonduplication claim.

\subsection{Non-claims}\label{saut:sec:nonclaims}

The source states its limitations where they arise, and each is kept in
place. The list collects them, with the place where each stands.
Items~1--28 are the source's; items~29--33 were added when the report
joined the collection. \mergetag\ The non-claims of the second source are listed
separately in Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:nonclaims}, and those of the third in
Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:nonclaims}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2.2em]
\item No complete classification of the automorphisms of $\K$, and in
particular none of the unrestricted ones, is claimed (title page, abstract,
Section~\ref{saut:sec:questions}; the delivered README).
\item Historical priority is not claimed or certified; derived statements
and useful examples are not automatically claimed to be first discoveries,
and no exhaustive nonduplication claim is made (title page,
Section~\ref{saut:sec:audit}; the delivered README).
\item No new Lean verification, no Lean code, and no independent build of
the repository are claimed; the implementation order describes proposed
work, not existing modules (title page, Section~\ref{saut:sec:repo},
Section~\ref{saut:sec:audit}).
\item The repository comparison is a selected-source inspection, not a
search of every file; the bridge-note facts are repository-reported, not
independently rebuilt (Section~\ref{saut:sec:repo}).
\item Exponential faithfulness, the rational non-lifting proposition and
the logarithmic-modulus reduction are prior work of the repository, not
results of this report; the settlement of Question~5.4 of \cite{KKS} is by
the repository's proof and ``is not a new result of this article''
(abstract, Sections~\ref{saut:sec:introduction}
and~\ref{saut:sec:exponential}, Section~\ref{saut:sec:audit}).
\item Faithfulness does not say that the exponential automorphism group is
trivial (Section~\ref{saut:sec:exponential}).
\item The article does not rely on the earlier absence claim in the
rigidity report's audit (Section~\ref{saut:sec:audit}).
\item $\Aut(\K)$ and the other groups are definable collections of class
maps, not class objects of NBG; a group isomorphism is a unique
factorization statement about individual automorphisms
(Section~\ref{saut:sec:introduction}).
\item Global homogeneity and the topology of the full field are not to be
transferred to an arbitrary fixed set-sized workspace
(Section~\ref{saut:sec:introduction}).
\item The four-layer decomposition \eqref{saut:eq:fourfactor} is not a
decomposition of all field automorphisms of $\K$, and its factor $\U$ is not
reduced to a finite list of coefficients (Section~\ref{saut:sec:decomposition}).
\item An arbitrary homomorphism $g\mapsto u_g$ into $1+\mm$ does not by itself
give a summable or surjective map; the fixed-shift flows illustrate the
automorphism--derivation connection without asserting that every formal
operator exponential is summable (Section~\ref{saut:sec:kernels}).
\item The Taylor motions $\Phi_d$ are generally noncomputable from
numerical names of real coefficients and are not to be advertised as
algorithms; the back-and-forth existence theorems are not executable
algorithms and give no canonical parametrization of the class
automorphisms (Sections~\ref{saut:sec:kernels}, \ref{saut:sec:bare}
and~\ref{saut:sec:applications}).
\item The maps of $G_{\No}$ are semilinearly compatible with the modulus;
calling them isometries would be misleading (Section~\ref{saut:sec:axis}).
\item Proposition~\ref{saut:prop:continuous} is an inclusion in the group of
fine-continuous automorphisms, not a classification of it, and no converse
is inferred from continuity; not every fine-continuous pure-field
automorphism is identified (Sections~\ref{saut:sec:topology}
and~\ref{saut:sec:questions}).
\item The non-strong witness of Section~\ref{saut:sec:topology} is not
claimed to fix every value or every leading term, nor to have the global
leading-term property asserted for the construction in \cite{KKS}; the
non-density conclusion concerns the pointwise-equality topology on
automorphisms only (Sections~\ref{saut:sec:topology}
and~\ref{saut:sec:audit}).
\item The choice of topology is part of each topological theorem; the
discreteness of set-sized subsets in the full fine topology says nothing
about the intrinsic valuation topology of a fixed Hahn workspace
(Section~\ref{saut:sec:topology}).
\item $S_2$ is not asserted to be representable by a locally convergent
power series; ``everywhere differentiable'' is not a synonym for analytic
rigidity, and a zero derivative does not imply local constancy
(Section~\ref{saut:sec:derivative}).
\item The scalar Berarducci--Mantova derivation, formal differentiation of a
power series and the fine derivative are three distinct notions; the
differential reduction is not a classification of the differential group
or of differential automorphisms without a marked real axis
(Section~\ref{saut:sec:exponential}).
\item The transcendence-basis description is not a classification, even for
a set-sized algebraically closed field (Section~\ref{saut:sec:bare}).
\item Neither the phase family nor
Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:inequivalentrealform} classifies the real closed
class fields whose complexification is $\K$; the conjugacy criterion of
Appendix~\ref{saut:app:conjugacy} is structural, not an effective decision
procedure (Section~\ref{saut:sec:realforms},
Appendix~\ref{saut:app:conjugacy}). \mergetag\ Still true; the valued,
$\Oz[i]$-preserving real forms are now classified
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:involutions}).
\item Simplicity rigidity is rigidity of an enriched structure, not of the
real closed field reduct (Section~\ref{saut:sec:exponential}).
\item Exact value rigidity for the omega-map needs pointwise value
fixation; it describes one kernel and is not a solution of the omega-map

resolved (Sections~\ref{saut:sec:exponential}
and~\ref{saut:sec:questions}).

compatibility with the total real exponential on infinite arguments
(Section~\ref{saut:sec:exponential}).
\item The image of exponential automorphisms in the value group is not
determined (Section~\ref{saut:sec:questions}).
\item Automorphism tests do not make the constructions they constrain

(Section~\ref{saut:sec:applications}).
\item Smallness statements keep their universe indices; the class theory
here and a universe-relative Lean realization are compatible but not

\item The delivered README reports symbolic finite checks of the norm
product and polarization identities, the Cayley inverse, and the
rational-flow composition and inverse identities, and says that such
checks are not proofs of infinite summability or of the full surreal
theory. No code or record of them was delivered, and nothing in this
report rests on them. The delivery redistributes no third-party papers or
font files.
\item \emph{(Added.)} The answer to Question~5.4 of \cite{KKS} rests on the

is exponential when $a$ is not rational (Section~\ref{saut:sec:conventions}).
\item \emph{(Added; status updated.)} No dedicated \texttt{saut:} mappings
are supplied. Existing generic Lean proofs cover ordered displacement,

Section~\ref{saut:sec:position}; their actual surreal exponential

\item \emph{(Added.)} The relations of Section~\ref{saut:sec:position}
identify results by their content; no theorem is transferred between the

\item \emph{(Added.)} Observation \eqref{saut:eq:Lchain} is an elementary

\subsection{The main picture}

The correct starting point is the strict separation
\[
 \{\id,c\}=\Aut(\K/\No)
 \ \subsetneq\ G_{\No}
 \ \subsetneq\ G_v
 \ \subsetneq\ \Aut(\K).
\]
Preserving the real axis gives $\Aut(\No)\times C_2$; preserving the unit
circle gives the same group. Exact preservation of every modulus reduces
it to identity and conjugation. Pure-field automorphisms can move the
real axis, the finite ring, and even the isomorphism type of a chosen real
form under a change of conjugation.

The valuation organizes a large explicit subgroup but does not enforce
classical analytic behavior. Phase twists can move real monomials while
fixing constants and values; leading-term automorphisms can move ordinary
real constants by infinitesimals; fine-continuous maps need not preserve
Hahn sums; and exponent dilation can be a differentiable field
homeomorphism with derivative zero everywhere. Exponential structure
imposes a different and genuinely strong constraint: its value-group
action is faithful, even though the underlying valued-field kernel is
large.

An automorphism theorem for surcomplex numbers is therefore meaningful only
after its preserved structure, support convention, topology, and size
framework have been made explicit. With those choices in place, the theory
has both exact structural reductions and a rich supply of concrete examples.

\section{Finite symmetry descent: statements and conventions}\label{saut:fs:sec:introduction}

Sections~\ref{saut:fs:sec:introduction}--\ref{saut:fs:sec:closing} print the
second source of this report, \emph{Finite Symmetry Descent and Real Forms of
Gaussian Omnific Integers} (Appendix~\ref{saut:app:source02}). Its text is kept
with the renamings of Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:conventions}; sentences added when
it was merged are marked \mergetag.

The field $\K=\No(i)$ is algebraically closed, whereas $\No$ is its standard real
closed subfield. Standard conjugation $c$ fixes $\No$ and restricts on $\Oz[i]$ to
an involution with fixed ring $\Oz$. This suggests a natural uniqueness problem:
how much additional structure must an involution preserve before its fixed pair
is necessarily a copy of $(\No,\Oz)$?

There are several different questions here. An automorphism may preserve only
the field operations, or additionally the natural Hahn valuation, the
coefficient field, the integer-support ring, or all infinite normal-form sums.
These conditions must not be conflated (Section~\ref{saut:sec:conventions}).
The literature develops detailed valuation and summation decompositions of Hahn
automorphism groups \cite{KS,KKS}. The collection already contains strong
monomial and phase symmetries and general surcomplex real forms
(Sections~\ref{saut:sec:explicit} and~\ref{saut:sec:realforms} and
Appendix~\ref{saut:app:conjugacy} of this report), and omnific-preserving
stabilizers (\repo{surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms}). In particular,
the existence of a nonstandard real form, without the additional conditions
imposed here, is not new (Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:inequivalentrealform}).

The contribution of the source is a simultaneous finite-symmetry normalization
and the resulting precise classification in an explicitly restricted category.
It also gives a strong negative answer to the uniqueness problem just posed:
even a genuine integer-part fixed ring does not force the standard real form.

\subsection{Conventions for the added sections}\label{saut:fs:sec:conventions}

The conventions of Section~\ref{saut:sec:conventions} stay in force: $v=\min\supp$,
$t^\gamma=\omega^{-\gamma}$, ``strong'' means that a map and its inverse preserve
set-indexed Hahn sums, a \emph{valued} automorphism maps the valuation ring onto
itself and may move values, and a \emph{value-fixing} one fixes every value.
Several letters of the source already have a meaning in this report, and some
of the source's letters carry two meanings. The source's symbols were therefore
renamed as follows; nothing else changed, and no normalization changed.

The symbols kept from the source are local to
Sections~\ref{saut:fs:sec:introduction}--\ref{saut:fs:sec:closing}:
$k$ is a set-sized coefficient field of characteristic zero, $\Gamma$ is a
set-sized divisible ordered abelian group or $\No$ (the set-based reading of
Section~\ref{saut:sec:introduction}), $K=k((t^\Gamma))$ is a full Hahn field (the
blackboard $\K$ remains $\No(i)$), $G$ is a finite group of automorphisms with
elements $g,h$ and $n=|G|$ (exponents are $\gamma,\delta$, never $g$), $m_\gamma$ is
the norm section, $\Ineg$ is the strictly negative-support part, $\OO=k+\mm$ is the
valuation ring of $K$, $\ct$ is the constant coefficient, $\mathcal N$ is a kernel
group, $F$ is a set-sized real closed \emph{coefficient} field, $\cc=2^{\aleph_0}$
(not the conjugation $c$), and $\psi$ is a local isomorphism of fields. In
Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:examples} the letters $z,u$ are the two monomials of
$K_{\mathrm{lex}}$, not a generic element.

The following readings are tempting and false.
\begin{itemize}
\item $\No_F=F((t^{\No}))$ is a field whose \emph{coefficient} field is $F$; the
subscript is not an inequality as in $\No_{<0}$. It is isomorphic to $\No$ only when
$F\cong\R$ (Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion}).
\item $R_F=\Z+F((t^{\No_{<0}}))$ is discretely ordered with least positive element
$1$ for every $F$, but it is an integer part of $\No_F$ only when $F$ is Archimedean
(Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:ip}). It is not ``the omnific integers of $\No_F$'' in
general.
\item $F((t^{\No_{<0}}))$ is a support-restricted additive part, not a Hahn field:
$\No_{<0}$ is not a group.
\item The coefficient field $F=\C^{\rho}$ of an involution is a real closed field of
transcendence degree $\cc$ in its intrinsic order; it need not be $\R$ and need not
lie on the usual real axis, although the ambient coefficient field is named $\C$
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:boundaries}).
\item $F_j=\Fix(j)$ of Section~\ref{saut:sec:realforms} is a class real form; for
$j\in\mathcal G$ it is isomorphic to $\No_F$ with $F=\C^{j|_\C}$, a set-sized
field. The two letters $F$ are never interchanged.
\item $\ct$ extends the residue map $\st$ of Section~\ref{saut:sec:hahn} to all of $K$
but is not a ring homomorphism on $K$.
\item A value-fixing automorphism need not fix leading terms: the conjugator
$\mathcal H$ is value-fixing, and generally not a $1$-automorphism.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{An explicit finite-symmetry normal form}

Small monomials are used throughout: $t^\gamma=\omega^{-\gamma}$ when $\Gamma=\No$.
A Hahn support is therefore well ordered, the valuation is its least exponent, and
purely infinite terms have \emph{negative} exponents. Let
\[
 K=k((t^\Gamma)),\qquad
 \Ineg=\{x\in K:\supp(x)\subseteq\Gamma_{<0}\}.
\]
The phrase \emph{strong automorphism} means preservation of set-indexed
Hahn-summable families and their sums in both directions.

\begin{theorem}[Finite symmetry descent; main statement]\label{saut:fs:thm:main-descent}
Let $k$ be a set-sized field of characteristic zero. Let $\Gamma$ be a set-sized
divisible ordered abelian group, or let $\Gamma=\No$ with set-supported series.
Suppose a finite group $G$ acts on $K=k((t^\Gamma))$ by valued automorphisms and
stabilizes $k$ and $\Ineg$. Write $n=|G|$ and $\rho_g=g|_k$. There is a strong
$k$-linear, value-fixing field automorphism $\mathcal H$ such that
\[
 \mathcal H(\Ineg)=\Ineg,\qquad \ct\circ\mathcal H=\ct,\qquad
 \mathcal H^{-1}g\mathcal H=M_{\rho_g}\quad(g\in G),
\]
where $M_{\rho_g}$ acts coefficientwise. One such automorphism is
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:main-norm}
 \mathcal H\left(\sum_\gamma a_\gamma t^\gamma\right)
 =\sum_\gamma a_\gamma\prod_{g\in G}g(t^{\gamma/n}).
\end{equation}
In particular, restriction to $k$ is faithful on $G$.
\end{theorem}

\mergetag\ The source assumes in addition that $G$ acts by \emph{strong} valued
automorphisms. That hypothesis follows from the others
(Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}, which is \lbl{opa:as:thm:ctiso} of
\repo{surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms}, placed after the source's pin), so
the theorem is printed without it; the proof in
Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:norm} uses strongness of the $g$ only through that
proposition. Source~03 (batch~34) proves the same conjugacy for strong actions that
need not stabilize $\Ineg$, over a field of any characteristic, with an explicit
inverse (Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:orbit}, Remark~\ref{saut:gr:rem:routes}).

The formula is a multiplicative norm, but not a norm of an arbitrary element
followed by an arbitrary root extraction. Dividing the exponent by $n$
\emph{before} taking the product supplies a coherent multiplicative section.
This is what makes the construction a field homomorphism rather than merely a
collection of invariant elements.

\subsection{The Gaussian omnific classification}

Put $\K=\No[i]=\C((t^{\No}))$ and let
\[
 \mathcal G=\{\alpha\in G_v:\alpha(\Oz[i])=\Oz[i]\}
\]
be the valued automorphisms of $\K$ that preserve $\Oz[i]$ setwise. A valued
automorphism is allowed to induce a nontrivial ordered automorphism of the exponent
group; it need not initially fix every value. \mergetag\ The source defines $\mathcal G$
as the \emph{strong} valued automorphisms preserving $\Oz[i]$. By
\lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian} of \repo{surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms}
(Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong} and Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:gaussianstrong}
here), every valued automorphism preserving $\Oz[i]$ is strong, so the two
collections coincide.

\begin{theorem}[Classification of admissible involutions]\label{saut:fs:thm:main-involutions}
For every nonidentity involution $j\in\mathcal G$, its restriction $\rho=j|_\C$ is a
nonidentity involution of $\C$. There is a $\C$-linear, strong, value-fixing
automorphism $\mathcal H_j$ preserving $\Oz[i]$ with
\[
 j=\mathcal H_jM_\rho\mathcal H_j^{-1},\qquad
 \mathcal H_j(t^\gamma)=t^{\gamma/2}j(t^{\gamma/2}).
\]
Two such involutions are conjugate in $\mathcal G$ if and only if their coefficient
involutions are conjugate in $\Aut(\C)$. Equivalently, their coefficient fixed
fields are isomorphic.

If $F=\C^\rho$, the fixed pair $(\Fix(j),\Fix(j)\cap\Oz[i])$ is isomorphic to
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:main-fixed-pair}
 (\No_F,R_F):=\left(F((t^{\No})),\;\Z+F((t^{\No_{<0}}))\right).
\end{equation}
The possible coefficient fields are exactly the real closed fields of
transcendence degree $\cc$ over $\Q$, up to isomorphism.
\end{theorem}

The notation $F((t^{\No_{<0}}))$ denotes a support-restricted additive part, not a
Hahn field: $\No_{<0}$ is not a group. This convention will always be explicit when
it occurs.

\begin{theorem}[Integer parts and the standard real form]\label{saut:fs:thm:main-ip}
For any set-sized real closed field $F$, $R_F$ is discretely ordered with least
positive element $1$. Moreover,
\[
 R_F\text{ is an integer part of }\No_F
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad F\text{ is Archimedean},
\]
and
\[
 \No_F\cong\No
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad F\cong\R.
\]
The field isomorphism in the second equivalence may be a plain field
isomorphism: real closed fields carry an intrinsically determined order.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Maximal set-sized multiplicity]\label{saut:fs:thm:main-count}
There are exactly $2^{\cc}$ conjugacy types of nonidentity involutions in
$\mathcal G$. There is a set-indexed family of $2^{\cc}$ representatives whose fixed
fields are pairwise nonisomorphic and whose fixed rings are genuine integer parts.
The representatives can all be chosen coefficientwise, hence strong and
value-fixing. Excluding the standard type still leaves $2^{\cc}$ types. These
representatives are pairwise nonconjugate even under arbitrary plain field
automorphisms of $\No[i]$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{What is, and is not, being resolved}\label{saut:fs:sec:resolved}

Theorems~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-descent}--\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-count} solve the
finite-action normalization and involution-classification problems \emph{under
their stated support and valuation hypotheses}. They are not asserted to settle a
named unrestricted question in the published literature. In particular, the source
does not prove that an arbitrary automorphism of $\Oz[i]$ preserves the valuation
or all Hahn sums. It does not classify all real forms of the plain class field
$\No[i]$, and it makes no claim about exponential compatibility.
\mergetag\ Status against the current collection: a \emph{valued} automorphism
preserving $\Oz[i]$ preserves all Hahn sums (\lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}); whether
every automorphism of $\Oz[i]$ is valued is \lbl{opa:as:q:gaussian}, still open.
For this report, Theorems~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-involutions}
and~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-count} answer the valued case of the real-form
classification left open in Section~\ref{saut:sec:questions}; the unrestricted
case stays open.

The proof is organized so that no proposed theorem from an unreviewed draft is an
indispensable premise. The needed reconstruction observations are reproved.
Classical maximality and algebraic-closure results are identified explicitly. The
proper-class argument is local and requires no large-cardinal assumption.

\section{Hahn fields, support rings, and size conventions}\label{saut:fs:sec:prelim}

\subsection{Normal forms and strong summation}

Let $\Gamma$ be an ordered abelian group and $k$ a field. A Hahn series is a formal
expression $x=\sum_{\gamma\in S}x_\gamma t^\gamma$, where $S\subseteq\Gamma$ is well
ordered and $x_\gamma\in k$. Its support is the subset on which $x_\gamma\ne0$.
Addition is coefficientwise and multiplication is convolution. If $S,S'$ are well
ordered, then $S+S'$ is well ordered and every fixed exponent has only finitely
many presentations as $s+s'$ with $s\in S$, $s'\in S'$
(Lemma~\ref{saut:lem:support}; \cite[Lemma~1]{Poonen}).

A set-indexed family $(x_j)_{j\in J}$ is \emph{Hahn summable} when the union of its
supports is well ordered and every exponent occurs in the support of only finitely
many members. Its sum is obtained coefficientwise. The definition is stronger than
a claim about convergence of a sequence in a particular topology. Uncountable index
sets are allowed, but never a proper-class-indexed sum.

\begin{definition}\label{saut:fs:def:strong}
An additive map is strong if it takes every Hahn-summable family to a Hahn-summable
family and commutes with its sum. A strong automorphism is a field automorphism for
which both it and its inverse are strong.
\end{definition}

(\mergetag\ This is the definition of Section~\ref{saut:sec:hahn}, for an arbitrary
coefficient field and exponent group.) The two-direction requirement is intentional. It agrees
with the care taken in the definition of strongly additive valued automorphisms in
\cite[Definition~4.0.5]{KS}. Below, strongness of the inverse will be proved, not
inferred merely from bijectivity.

For $x\ne0$, put $v(x)=\min\supp(x)$ and $v(0)=\infty$. A valued automorphism
preserves the valuation ring and hence induces an ordered automorphism of $\Gamma$.
A value-fixing automorphism $\alpha$ satisfies $v(\alpha x)=v(x)$ for all $x$.

\subsection{Three support regions}

Write
\begin{align*}
 \Ineg&=\{x:\supp(x)\subseteq\Gamma_{<0}\},&
 \mm&=\{x:\supp(x)\subseteq\Gamma_{>0}\},\\
 A_k&=k+\Ineg,& \OO&=k+\mm.
\end{align*}
Then $A_k$ and $\OO$ are rings, $\Ineg$ is an ideal of $A_k$, $\mm$ is the maximal
ideal of $\OO$, and
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:three-parts}
 K=\Ineg\oplus k\oplus\mm
\end{equation}
as additive groups and as $k$-vector spaces. The zero coefficient is denoted
$\ct(x)$. It is a ring homomorphism on each of $A_k$ and $\OO$, but \emph{not} on all
of $K$: for example, $\ct(t^\gamma)=\ct(t^{-\gamma})=0$ while
$\ct(t^\gamma t^{-\gamma})=1$ when $\gamma\ne0$.

For a unital subring $\mathfrak o\subseteq k$, define
\[
 A_{\mathfrak o}=\mathfrak o+\Ineg.
\]
The distinction between $A_k$ and $\OO$ is essential: one has only nonpositive
support, and the other only nonnegative support.

\subsection{The surreal identification}

Conway normal forms give the identifications \eqref{saut:eq:hahn}, $\No=\R((t^{\No}))$
and $\No[i]=\C((t^{\No}))$ with set-sized supports. In this identification,
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:omnific-hahn}
 \Oz=\Z+\Ineg_{\R},\qquad
 \Oz[i]=\Z[i]+\Ineg_{\C}.
\end{equation}
Here an omnific integer has no negative powers of $\omega$, and its constant
coefficient is an ordinary integer. The standard foundations are Conway's and
Gonshor's accounts \cite{Conway,Gonshor}; the overview \cite{Wiki} supplied with the
source is useful for orientation, not as a substitute for those foundations.

For a real closed $F$, the field $F((t^\Gamma))$ with divisible $\Gamma$ is real
closed. One way to see the precise input is to use \cite[Corollary~4]{Poonen}:
$F(i)((t^\Gamma))$ is algebraically closed, and it equals $F((t^\Gamma))(i)$. The
order is determined by the sign of the leading coefficient. For $\Gamma=\No$, each
polynomial involves a set of exponents and lies in a set-sized divisible-group Hahn
subfield, so the same conclusion follows locally.

\subsection{Class conventions}

Set-sized statements are in ZFC. The proper-class statements may be read in GBC
with global choice (the framework of Section~\ref{saut:sec:introduction}), with all
displayed automorphisms understood as class maps and all supports and families
understood to be sets. Only replacement for images of sets, elementary class
comprehension, and set-sized field arguments are used in the construction. There
is no appeal to a class-sized transcendence basis or to Zorn's lemma on a proper
class.

The notation $\mathcal G$ for a collection of class automorphisms is convenient
second-order shorthand, like $\Aut(\K)$ in Section~\ref{saut:sec:introduction}. The
counting theorem means that there is a uniform family indexed by a set of size
$2^{\cc}$ meeting every admissible conjugacy type exactly once. It does not assert
that class automorphisms are sets or elements of an ordinary set-theoretic quotient
group.

Every set of surreal exponents has an upper and a lower bound in $\No$. This
elementary set-cut property will be used for denominator clearing. It must not be
confused with an analogous assertion for a fixed set-sized ordered exponent group,
where it can fail.

\section{A coefficient-pairing test for summability}\label{saut:fs:sec:pairing}

A technical point in the main proof is that a constructed bijective strong map need
not automatically have a strong inverse. The following elementary criterion
supplies the missing implication. The source includes a complete proof as a
supporting tool and attaches no priority claim to the criterion.
\mergetag\ Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:pairing} is \lbl{opa:as:thm:detect} and
Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:ct-strong} is the case $\alpha=\id$ of
\lbl{opa:as:thm:ctiso}, both in \repo{surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms}
and both placed after the source's pin. That report proves more: it suffices to
test countably supported $y$ with coefficients in $\{0,1\}$, it gives three routes
(a triangular detector, a binary finite-row lemma, and an induced matching), and
it treats isomorphisms between different Hahn fields that are semilinear over a
coefficient isomorphism. The Baire-category route below is a fourth route. It
quantifies over all $y$, so it proves the weaker form of the criterion.

\begin{lemma}[Row-finite uniformity]\label{saut:fs:lem:row-finite}
Let $k$ be a field and let $(f_j)_{j\in\N}$ be linear functionals on $k^{\N}$, each
depending on only finitely many coordinates. If for every $b\in k^{\N}$ only
finitely many $f_j(b)$ are nonzero, then only finitely many $f_j$ are nonzero
functionals.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Give $k$ the discrete topology and $k^{\N}$ the complete product metric in which two
different sequences have distance $2^{-r}$ when $r$ is their first differing
coordinate. For each $N$, the set
\[
 Z_N=\bigcap_{j\ge N}\ker f_j
\]
is closed. The hypothesis says $\bigcup_NZ_N=k^{\N}$. By the Baire category theorem,
some $Z_N$ contains a nonempty basic cylinder, which fixes only coordinates
$0,\ldots,r-1$.

For $j\ge N$, varying any coordinate beyond this prefix inside the cylinder shows
that $f_j$ has zero coefficient at that coordinate. Thus all those $f_j$ depend only
on the same finite prefix. Apply the hypothesis separately to each of its coordinate
vectors $e_0,\ldots,e_{r-1}$. Beyond a common finite index, every $f_j(e_s)$
vanishes. Such an $f_j$ is the zero functional.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Constant-term pairing criterion]\label{saut:fs:thm:pairing}
Let $K=k((t^\Gamma))$ be a full Hahn field, with either a set-sized exponent group or
set-supported class exponents. A set-indexed family $(x_i)_{i\in J}$ is Hahn summable
if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:pairing-test}
 \text{for every }y\in K,\quad
 \{i\in J:\ct(x_i y)\ne0\}\text{ is finite}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $(x_i)$ is summable, multiplying it by the single series $y$ gives a summable
family. At exponent zero only finitely many members contribute, which proves
\eqref{saut:fs:eq:pairing-test}.

Conversely, if a fixed exponent $\gamma$ occurs in infinitely many $x_i$, use
$y=t^{-\gamma}$ to contradict the test. We may therefore assume point-finiteness of
the support family. If the union $S$ of the supports were not well ordered, it
would contain a strictly decreasing sequence $\gamma_0>\gamma_1>\cdots$. Each
individual support meets this sequence only finitely often, since it is well
ordered. Passing to a subsequence, choose distinct indices $i_j$ with
$(x_{i_j})_{\gamma_j}\ne0$.

For arbitrary $b=(b_j)\in k^{\N}$, the expression
\[
 y_b=\sum_{j\ge0}b_jt^{-\gamma_j}
\]
is a Hahn series, because $-\gamma_j$ is strictly increasing. For every selected row,
the expression
\[
 f_r(b)=\ct(x_{i_r}y_b)=\sum_{j\ge0}(x_{i_r})_{\gamma_j}b_j
\]
is a finite linear form. There are infinitely many nonzero forms, since $f_r$ has a
nonzero coefficient at $b_r$. The pairing test says that every $b$ makes only
finitely many of them nonzero, contradicting Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:row-finite}.
Thus $S$ is well ordered, and the family is summable.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Constant-term preserving isomorphisms are strong]\label{saut:fs:cor:ct-strong}
Suppose $T:K\to K$ is a $k$-linear field automorphism and $\ct(Tx)=\ct(x)$ for every
$x\in K$. Then both $T$ and $T^{-1}$ are strong.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For all $x,y$,
\[
 \ct((Tx)y)=\ct\bigl(T(xT^{-1}y)\bigr)=\ct(xT^{-1}y).
\]
The pairing criterion and the surjectivity of $T$ show that $(x_i)$ is summable
exactly when $(Tx_i)$ is summable. Put $x=\sum_i x_i$ and $z=\sum_iTx_i$. For every
$y$,
\[
 \ct((Tx)y)=\ct(xT^{-1}y)=\sum_i\ct(x_iT^{-1}y)=\sum_i\ct((Tx_i)y)=\ct(zy).
\]
The sums of coefficients here are finite. The pairing is nondegenerate: if $w\ne0$,
choose any nonzero coefficient $w_\gamma$ and use $y=t^{-\gamma}$. Hence $Tx=z$. The
same argument applies to $T^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{saut:fs:rem:monomialtests}
The test quantifies over \emph{all} series $y$, not only monomials. Monomial tests
detect point-finiteness but do not detect a descending sequence in the union of
supports. That second obstruction is exactly what the row-finite argument handles.
(\mergetag\ \lbl{opa:as:ex:monomialtests} is an explicit instance.)
\end{remark}

The corollary is stated for $k$-linear maps. \mergetag\ Composed with a
coefficientwise lift it gives a semilinear form, and with it the strongness
hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-descent} becomes redundant.

\begin{proposition}[Automatic strongness; \mergetag]\label{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}
Let $K=k((t^\Gamma))$ be a full Hahn field, $\Gamma$ a set or $\No$ with set supports,
and let $g$ be a valued field automorphism of $K$ with $g(k)=k$ and $g(\Ineg)=\Ineg$.
Put $\rho=g|_k$. Then $\ct(gx)=\rho(\ct x)$ for every $x$, $g$ is $\rho$-semilinear,
and $g$ and $g^{-1}$ are strong. No divisibility of $\Gamma$ and no hypothesis on the
characteristic is needed.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Since $g(\OO)=\OO$, also $g(\mm)=\mm$. Writing $x=p+a+\varepsilon$ according to
\eqref{saut:fs:eq:three-parts}, $gx=gp+ga+g\varepsilon$ with $gp\in\Ineg$,
$ga=\rho(a)\in k$ and $g\varepsilon\in\mm$, so $\ct(gx)=\rho(\ct x)$; semilinearity
is $g(ax)=\rho(a)g(x)$. The coefficientwise lift $M_{\rho}$ is a strong
automorphism with $\ct\circ M_\rho^{-1}=\rho^{-1}\circ\ct$. Hence $T=M_\rho^{-1}g$
is a $k$-linear field automorphism with $\ct\circ T=\ct$, and
Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:ct-strong} makes $T$ and $T^{-1}$ strong. So are
$g=M_\rho T$ and $g^{-1}=T^{-1}M_\rho^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

This is \lbl{opa:as:thm:ctiso} with $F=g$ and $\alpha=\rho$, together with the
three-region argument of the proof of \lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}; it is printed here
because the source's hypotheses and Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:strong} depend on it.
It is an observation of the merge, not a result of the source.

\section{Maximality and the local treatment of class exponents}\label{saut:fs:sec:maximal}

\subsection{The set-sized maximality input}

A valued-field extension is immediate if it has the same value group and residue
field. A maximally complete valued field has no proper immediate extension. Full
Hahn fields are maximally complete \cite[Theorem~1]{Poonen}. For clarity, the
source gives the standard support proof in the form needed here.

\begin{lemma}[Spherical completeness of full Hahn fields]\label{saut:fs:lem:spherical}
For set-sized $k$ and $\Gamma$, every nest of nonempty closed valuation balls in
$k((t^\Gamma))$ has nonempty intersection.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A ball $\mathbb B(a,r)=\{x:v(x-a)\ge r\}$ prescribes the coefficients at all
exponents $\gamma<r$ to equal those of $a$. In a nest, these prescriptions are
compatible. Define $x_\gamma$ by a prescription whenever some ball prescribes that
exponent, and set it to zero otherwise.

Its nonzero support $S$ is well ordered. Indeed, given a nonempty subset
$S'\subseteq S$, choose $\gamma_0\in S'$. Some ball prescribes the coefficient at
$\gamma_0$, so its radius is greater than $\gamma_0$. Every nonzero prescribed
coefficient at an exponent at most $\gamma_0$ then agrees with the center of that
ball. The nonempty set $S'\cap(-\infty,\gamma_0]$ is a subset of its well-ordered
support, and its least element is also the least element of $S'$. Thus $x$ is a
Hahn series. By construction it belongs to every ball in the nest.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{saut:fs:lem:immediate}
A spherically complete valued field has no proper immediate extension.
Consequently, a value-fixing self-embedding of a full set-sized Hahn field which
induces the identity on its residue field is surjective.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $K_1/K_0$ were proper and immediate, and choose $a\in K_1\setminus K_0$. For
every $w\in K_0$, immediacy provides $d\in K_0$ with $v(a-d)>v(a-w)$: first match
the value of $a-w$ by an element of $K_0$, then match its leading residue. Thus the
set $\{v(a-w):w\in K_0\}$ has no maximum.

The balls $\mathbb B(w,v(a-w))$ in $K_0$ form a nest. If $b$ lay in their
intersection, the ultrametric inequality would give $v(a-b)\ge v(a-w)$ for all $w$,
contradicting the absence of a maximum. This proves maximality. For the final
assertion, the image of the self-embedding is isometric to the full Hahn field and
hence maximal; the ambient field is an immediate extension of that image and cannot
be larger.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Why a separate class argument is necessary}

A proper-class Hahn field must not be declared maximal by repeating a set-sized
proof with an unexamined class of balls. The following lemma avoids that issue
entirely.

\begin{lemma}[Invariant set-sized exponent closure]\label{saut:fs:lem:local}
Let a finite group $G$ act strongly and valuedly on $k((t^{\No}))$, stabilizing $k$.
For any set $S\subseteq\No$, there is a set-sized divisible subgroup
$\Delta\subseteq\No$ containing $S$ such that every $g\in G$ restricts to an
automorphism of $k((t^\Delta))$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The induced action of $G$ on the ordered group $\No$ is trivial: a finite-order
increasing permutation of a linear order fixes every point. Indeed,
$\tau(\gamma)>\gamma$ or $\tau(\gamma)<\gamma$ would yield a strict chain under
iteration which could not return to $\gamma$.

Let $\Delta_0$ be the divisible subgroup generated by $S$. Given the set-sized
divisible subgroup $\Delta_r$, let $\Delta_{r+1}$ be the divisible subgroup
generated by
\[
 \Delta_r\ \cup\!\bigcup_{g\in G,\ \gamma\in\Delta_r}\supp\bigl(g(t^\gamma)\bigr).
\]
Replacement and union show that it is still a set. The union
$\Delta=\bigcup_{r<\omega}\Delta_r$ is set-sized and divisible. Every $g(t^\gamma)$
with $\gamma\in\Delta$ has support in $\Delta$. Strongness and $k$-stability
therefore give $g(k((t^\Delta)))\subseteq k((t^\Delta))$. Since $g^{-1}$ is also in
$G$, equality holds.
\end{proof}

The closure is adapted to the specified finite action and to the element under
consideration. It does not assert the existence of a single set-sized subfield
containing the whole class field or all its automorphisms. \mergetag\ When $G$ also
stabilizes $\Ineg$, strongness is automatic
(Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}).

\section{Proof of finite symmetry descent}\label{saut:fs:sec:norm}

We now prove Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-descent}. By
Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong} every $g\in G$ is strong. Let $G$ have
order $n$ and write
\[
 \rho_g=g|_k,\qquad m_\gamma=\prod_{g\in G}g(t^{\gamma/n}).
\]
The trivial-group case is included: then $n=1$ and $m_\gamma=t^\gamma$.

\subsection{The invariant section}

\begin{lemma}\label{saut:fs:lem:section}
For all $\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma$ and $h\in G$,
\[
 m_0=1,\quad m_{\gamma+\delta}=m_\gamma m_\delta,
 \quad v(m_\gamma)=\gamma,\quad h(m_\gamma)=m_\gamma.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every element of the induced finite group of ordered automorphisms of $\Gamma$ is
the identity, by the argument in Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:local}. Thus every $g$ fixes
values. Divisibility and torsion-freeness give the unique exponent $\gamma/n$.
Multiplicativity follows by applying each $g$ to
$t^{(\gamma+\delta)/n}=t^{\gamma/n}t^{\delta/n}$ and multiplying. The valuation of
the product is the sum of its $n$ values, hence $\gamma$. Finally, left
multiplication by $h$ permutes $G$, so it permutes the factors of $m_\gamma$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Diagonal summability}

\begin{lemma}\label{saut:fs:lem:diagonal}
For every well-ordered set $S\subseteq\Gamma$, the family $(m_\gamma)_{\gamma\in S}$
is Hahn summable.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For a fixed $g$, the family $(t^{\gamma/n})_{\gamma\in S}$ is summable, and
strongness of $g$ makes $(g(t^{\gamma/n}))_{\gamma\in S}$ summable. Taking the
product of these finitely many summable families gives a summable family indexed by
$S^G$. This uses the ordinary finite-product Hahn support lemma.

The family $(m_\gamma)_{\gamma\in S}$ is its diagonal subfamily, corresponding to
tuples all of whose coordinates are $\gamma$. Every subfamily of a summable family is
summable.
\end{proof}

Define
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:H-definition}
 \mathcal H(x)=\mathcal H\left(\sum_\gamma x_\gamma t^\gamma\right)
 :=\sum_\gamma x_\gamma m_\gamma.
\end{equation}
The preceding lemma makes the definition meaningful for every $x$. It also proves
forward strongness. More explicitly, for a summable family $(x_j)$, put
$S=\bigcup_j\supp(x_j)$. The family $(m_\gamma)_{\gamma\in S}$ is summable, and for
each $\gamma$ only finitely many coefficients $(x_j)_\gamma$ are nonzero.
Consequently, the doubly indexed family $((x_j)_\gamma m_\gamma)_{j,\gamma}$ is
summable. Regrouping it proves both summability of $(\mathcal H(x_j))$ and the
equality $\mathcal H(\sum_jx_j)=\sum_j\mathcal H(x_j)$.

\subsection{Field embedding and value preservation}

\begin{lemma}\label{saut:fs:lem:embedding}
The map $\mathcal H$ is a strong $k$-linear field embedding, fixes $k$ pointwise, and
satisfies $v(\mathcal Hx)=v(x)$ for every $x$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Linearity and $\mathcal H(1)=1$ are immediate. For products, the ordinary Hahn
convolution and Lemmas~\ref{saut:fs:lem:section} and~\ref{saut:fs:lem:diagonal}
justify
\[
 \mathcal H(x)\mathcal H(y)
 =\sum_{\gamma,\delta}x_\gamma y_\delta m_\gamma m_\delta
 =\sum_{\gamma,\delta}x_\gamma y_\delta m_{\gamma+\delta}
 =\mathcal H(xy).
\]
If $\gamma_0=v(x)$, the term $x_{\gamma_0}m_{\gamma_0}$ has value $\gamma_0$, whereas
every other term has strictly larger value. Its nonzero leading coefficient cannot
cancel. Thus $v(\mathcal Hx)=\gamma_0$, which also proves injectivity. Since $m_0=1$,
$\mathcal H$ fixes $k$.
\end{proof}

The leading coefficient of $m_\gamma$ need not be $1$. Thus $\mathcal H$ is
value-fixing, but it need not be a leading-term-fixing automorphism. This minor
distinction is necessary even for ordinary phase characters
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:examples}).

\subsection{Surjectivity: sets first, then classes}

For a set-sized $\Gamma$, the image $\mathcal H(K)$ is a maximally complete valued
field by Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:immediate}. It has value group $\Gamma$ and contains
$k$, with residue field $k$. The extension $K/\mathcal H(K)$ is immediate; hence
$\mathcal H(K)=K$.

For $\Gamma=\No$, fix a target $y\in K$ and apply Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:local} to
$S=\supp(y)$. On $k((t^\Delta))$, all the automorphisms $g$ restrict, and the very
same formula \eqref{saut:fs:eq:H-definition} defines a value-fixing self-embedding of
that full set-sized Hahn field. By the preceding paragraph it is surjective. In
particular, $y$ has a preimage in $k((t^\Delta))$. Since $y$ was arbitrary, the class
map $\mathcal H$ is surjective.

This step is the reason for using a full Hahn field. The construction of a summable
invariant section by itself proves an embedding, not yet an isomorphism on an
arbitrary smaller series field.

\subsection{The negative-support part and the inverse}

If $\gamma<0$, then $t^{\gamma/n}\in\Ineg$ and each $g(t^{\gamma/n})\in\Ineg$. The
product $m_\gamma$ is again in $\Ineg$. By strong summation,
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:H-I-inclusion}
 \mathcal H(\Ineg)\subseteq\Ineg.
\end{equation}
Value preservation and surjectivity give $\mathcal H(\mm)=\mm$. To prove equality in
\eqref{saut:fs:eq:H-I-inclusion}, write the preimage of $p\in\Ineg$ as
$x=q+a+\varepsilon$ according to \eqref{saut:fs:eq:three-parts}. Then
\[
 p=\mathcal H(q)+a+\mathcal H(\varepsilon),\qquad
 \mathcal H(q)\in\Ineg,\quad \mathcal H(\varepsilon)\in\mm.
\]
The direct-sum decomposition forces $a=0$ and $\mathcal H(\varepsilon)=0$. Thus
$x\in\Ineg$, proving $\mathcal H(\Ineg)=\Ineg$.

The same decomposition now shows $\ct(\mathcal Hx)=\ct(x)$ for every $x$. By
Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:ct-strong}, the inverse $\mathcal H^{-1}$ is strong. This
proves all the structural properties asserted for $\mathcal H$.

\subsection{Intertwining and fixed points}

For $h\in G$, strongness and invariance of $m_\gamma$ give
\[
 h\mathcal H\left(\sum_\gamma a_\gamma t^\gamma\right)
 =\sum_\gamma\rho_h(a_\gamma)m_\gamma
 =\mathcal HM_{\rho_h}\left(\sum_\gamma a_\gamma t^\gamma\right).
\]
Therefore $\mathcal H^{-1}h\mathcal H=M_{\rho_h}$. If $\rho_h=\id$, this identity and
surjectivity imply $h=\id$. This completes the proof of
Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-descent}.

\begin{corollary}[Fixed fields and support rings]\label{saut:fs:cor:fixed-general}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-descent}, suppose also that
$G$ stabilizes a unital subring $\mathfrak o\subseteq k$. Then
\[
 K^G=\mathcal H\bigl(k^G((t^\Gamma))\bigr),\qquad
 A_{\mathfrak o}^G=\mathcal H\bigl(\mathfrak o^G+\Ineg_{k^G}\bigr).
\]
The conjugating map fixes $\mathfrak o$ pointwise and preserves $A_{\mathfrak o}$
setwise.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A series is fixed by every coefficientwise $M_{\rho_g}$ exactly when each of its
coefficients belongs to $k^G$. Intersecting with $\mathfrak o+\Ineg$ restricts the
constant coefficient to $\mathfrak o^G$. The properties of $\mathcal H$ proved above
transport these identities to the original action.
\end{proof}

\section{Finite lifts, conjugacy, and torsion}\label{saut:fs:sec:group}

\subsection{All finite lifts of a given coefficient action}

Let $\mathcal N$ be the strong $k$-linear, value-fixing automorphisms of $K$
preserving $\Ineg$. (\mergetag\ By Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:ct-strong}, ``strong''
is automatic here: such a map preserves $\Ineg$, $k$ and $\mm$, hence $\ct$.) A
coefficient automorphism $\rho\in\Aut(k)$ has a canonical strong lift $M_\rho$,
which normalizes $\mathcal N$.

\begin{corollary}[Conjugacy of finite lifts]\label{saut:fs:cor:lifts}
Let $\Theta$ be a finite group and let $\rho:\Theta\to\Aut(k)$ be an action.
Suppose $\alpha,\alpha':\Theta\to\Aut(K)$ are valued actions stabilizing $k$ and
$\Ineg$, each inducing $\rho$ on $k$. Then $\mathcal H_{\alpha'}\mathcal
H_\alpha^{-1}\in\mathcal N$ and
\[
 \alpha'_p=(\mathcal H_{\alpha'}\mathcal H_\alpha^{-1})\,\alpha_p\,
 (\mathcal H_{\alpha'}\mathcal H_\alpha^{-1})^{-1}\qquad(p\in\Theta).
\]
If $\rho$ is faithful, these are embeddings of $\Theta$ and all lifts of the
specified coefficient action are conjugate by $\mathcal N$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The norm proof applies to a finite acting group even when the action is not
faithful: the product is taken over its $|\Theta|$ elements, with repetitions of the
same automorphism allowed. It gives $\mathcal H_\alpha,\mathcal H_{\alpha'}\in\mathcal
N$ such that $\alpha_p=\mathcal H_\alpha M_{\rho_p}\mathcal H_\alpha^{-1}$ and
$\alpha'_p=\mathcal H_{\alpha'}M_{\rho_p}\mathcal H_{\alpha'}^{-1}$; compose.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Torsion-free coefficient kernel]\label{saut:fs:cor:torsion}
Every finite-order valued automorphism of $K$ stabilizing $k$ and $\Ineg$ and fixing
$k$ pointwise is the identity. In particular, $\mathcal N$ is torsion-free.
\end{corollary}

(As in Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-descent}, the source's word ``strong'' in the
two corollaries is dropped by Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}; \mergetag.)
For $K=\K$ and $k=\C$, Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:torsion} is contained in
Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:torsion}, which proves $\Aut(\K/\C)$ torsion-free with no
valuation hypothesis (\mergetag); for other coefficient fields the statement is the
source's.

There is also a precise cohomological formulation, without introducing an extra
existence assumption. Let $\Theta$ act on $\mathcal N$ by conjugation with
$M_{\rho_p}$. A cocycle $\xi_p\in\mathcal N$ satisfies
\[
 \xi_{pq}=\xi_p\bigl(M_{\rho_p}\xi_qM_{\rho_p}^{-1}\bigr).
\]
Then $\alpha_p=\xi_pM_{\rho_p}$ is a finite action of the required kind. The theorem
gives $\mathcal H\in\mathcal N$ with
\[
 \xi_p=\mathcal HM_{\rho_p}\mathcal H^{-1}M_{\rho_p}^{-1}.
\]
Thus the associated nonabelian $\mathrm H^1(\Theta,\mathcal N)$ is trivial. For class
fields, this is a shorthand assertion about each set-indexed finite cocycle, not an
unexamined construction of a set of all class cocycles.

\subsection{What the theorem does not say about infinite groups}

A finite orbit permits an invariant product with exactly $n$ factors and a coherent
exponent division by $n$. Neither operation has an automatic analogue for an
infinite group. The theorem therefore gives no classification of infinite subgroup
actions, no assertion that the entire automorphism group is conjugate to a
coefficient group, and no vanishing theorem for cohomology of arbitrary infinite
groups.

\section{Recovering coefficients from the Gaussian omnific ring}\label{saut:fs:sec:recovery}

To apply the descent theorem to $\Oz[i]$, coefficient stability and
$\Ineg$-stability should not be imposed silently. They follow from $\Oz[i]$ itself.
The reconstruction package is already present in the collection's omnific reports:
$\Ineg=\bigcap_nnA_{\mathfrak o}$ is \lbl{opa:lem:divisible} of
\repo{surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms}, the multiplier identity and its units
are \lbl{odg:def:thm:multiplier}, and $\Frac\Oz=\No$ is \lbl{odg:thm:fractions}, both
of \repo{surreal/omnific-diophantine-geometry}. The short arguments below are included
to make the application independent of their proof status; the source claims no
novelty for them.

\begin{proposition}[Intrinsic support ideal and coefficient field]\label{saut:fs:prop:reconstruction}
Let $k$ be a set-sized characteristic-zero field and let $\mathfrak o\subseteq k$ be
either $\Z$, or $\Z[i]$ with $k$ containing $i$. For $K=k((t^{\No}))$,
$\Ineg=\Ineg_k$, and $A_{\mathfrak o}=\mathfrak o+\Ineg$, one has
\begin{align}
 \Frac(A_{\mathfrak o})&=K,\label{saut:fs:eq:frac}\\
 \Ineg&=\bigcap_{n\ge1}nA_{\mathfrak o},\label{saut:fs:eq:intersection}\\
 A_k=k+\Ineg&=(\Ineg:\Ineg):=\{x\in K:x\Ineg\subseteq\Ineg\},\label{saut:fs:eq:multiplier}\\
 k&=A_k^\times\cup\{0\}.\label{saut:fs:eq:units-recover}
\end{align}
Every unital automorphism of $A_{\mathfrak o}$ extends uniquely to $K$ and preserves
$\Ineg$, $A_k$, and $k$ setwise.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Given $x\in K$, its support is a set of surreal exponents. Choose $\delta>0$ larger
than every exponent in that support. Then $t^{-\delta}\in\Ineg$ and
$t^{-\delta}x\in\Ineg$, so $x=(t^{-\delta}x)/t^{-\delta}$ lies in
$\Frac(A_{\mathfrak o})$.

The ideal $\Ineg$ is divisible by every nonzero ordinary integer. Conversely, if
$a+p\in\bigcap_{n\ge1}nA_{\mathfrak o}$, with $a\in\mathfrak o$, then $a\in n\mathfrak o$
for every $n$. The only such integer or Gaussian integer is zero. This proves
\eqref{saut:fs:eq:intersection}.

An element supported at nonpositive exponents multiplies strictly negative support
into strictly negative support. If $x$ has a nonzero coefficient at an exponent
$\epsilon>0$, choose $0<\delta<\epsilon$. The element $t^{-\delta}\in\Ineg$ makes
$xt^{-\delta}$ have a nonzero coefficient at $\epsilon-\delta>0$, so
$x\notin(\Ineg:\Ineg)$. This proves \eqref{saut:fs:eq:multiplier}.

For nonzero $x,y\in A_k$ with $xy=1$, both $v(x)$ and $v(y)$ are nonpositive, and
their sum is zero. Hence both valuations are zero. A series supported at nonpositive
exponents with minimum exponent zero is a constant. Thus $A_k^\times=k^\times$,
proving \eqref{saut:fs:eq:units-recover}. All four constructions are preserved by a
ring automorphism, after its unique extension to the fraction field.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The valuation is not recovered by this proposition]\label{saut:fs:rem:valuation}
The proposition recovers the coefficient field and the strictly negative-support
ideal. It does \emph{not} assert that every abstract ring automorphism preserves
the Hahn valuation ring $\OO$. The source adds: nor does it assert strongness, and
both remain explicit hypotheses in the definition of $\mathcal G$. \mergetag\ The
second half is now stale: once the valuation ring is preserved, strongness follows
(Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:gaussianstrong}). Preservation of $\OO$ remains a hypothesis
(\lbl{opa:as:q:gaussian}).
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Valued Gaussian automorphisms are strong; \mergetag]\label{saut:fs:rem:gaussianstrong}
Let $\alpha$ be a valued automorphism of $K=k((t^{\No}))$ preserving $A_{\mathfrak o}$,
with $\mathfrak o$ as in Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:reconstruction}. By that
proposition $\alpha(k)=k$ and $\alpha(\Ineg)=\Ineg$, and
Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong} makes $\alpha$ strong and
$\alpha|_k$-semilinear. For $k=\C$ and $\mathfrak o=\Z[i]$ this is
\lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}, whose proof is the same. Hence $\mathcal G$ is the class
the source calls $\mathcal G$. For arbitrary characteristic-zero $k$ and
$\mathfrak o\in\{\Z,\Z[i]\}$ it is a small piece of information on
\lbl{opa:as:q:otherrings}, which asks when preserving such a ring forces strongness;
there the valuation is not assumed, and the question is not answered.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}\label{saut:fs:cor:recover-pairs}
For set-sized real closed fields $F,F'$, any unital ring isomorphism $R_F\cong R_{F'}$
induces an isomorphism $F\cong F'$. Conversely, an isomorphism $F\cong F'$ extends
coefficientwise to an isomorphism of the pairs $(\No_F,R_F)\cong(\No_{F'},R_{F'})$.
Thus
\[
 R_F\cong R_{F'}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad F\cong F'.
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:reconstruction} with $\mathfrak o=\Z$ on each side.
The converse uses the coefficientwise extension and the identity on exponents.
\end{proof}

This corollary is useful even when the coefficient fields are non-Archimedean: the
abstract fixed ring remembers the whole coefficient field, not merely its natural
residue field.

\section{Classification of Gaussian-preserving involutions}\label{saut:fs:sec:involutions}

Let $\K=\No[i]=\C((t^{\No}))$ and let $c$ be standard conjugation. Recall that
$\mathcal G$ consists of the valued automorphisms preserving $\Oz[i]$. It is closed
under composition and inverse.

\subsection{Reduction to coefficient conjugation}

Let $j\in\mathcal G$ have order two. By
Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:reconstruction}, $j$ stabilizes $\C$ and $\Ineg$. The
finite descent theorem applies to $\{1,j\}$. It yields
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:involution-H}
 \mathcal H_j(t^\gamma)=t^{\gamma/2}j(t^{\gamma/2}),\qquad
 j=\mathcal H_jM_\rho\mathcal H_j^{-1},
 \quad \rho=j|_\C.
\end{equation}
If $\rho$ were the identity, so would be $j$. Thus $\rho$ is a nontrivial involution
of $\C$.

The classical Artin--Schreier theorem, applied in the ordinary set field $\C$, says
that $F=\C^\rho$ is real closed and $\C=F(i)$, with $\rho(i)=-i$ \cite{Conrad}.
Consequently $\Z[i]^\rho=\Z$, and Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:fixed-general}
identifies the fixed pair with $(\No_F,R_F)$ in \eqref{saut:fs:eq:main-fixed-pair}.

No extension of the Artin--Schreier theorem to a proper-class Galois theory is
needed in this step. Once the coefficient theorem is known, the class fixed field is
obtained directly, coefficient by coefficient. In particular, every $z\in\K$ can also
be written uniquely as $x+iy$ with $x,y\in\Fix(j)$ after transporting the
coefficientwise decomposition through $\mathcal H_j$. (\mergetag\ That $\Fix(j)$ is a
real form also follows from Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:torsion}, for every involution.)

\subsection{Conjugacy is exactly coefficient conjugacy}

\begin{proposition}\label{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy}
For nonidentity involutions $j_1,j_2\in\mathcal G$, the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $j_1$ and $j_2$ are conjugate in $\mathcal G$;
\item $\rho_1=j_1|_\C$ and $\rho_2=j_2|_\C$ are conjugate in $\Aut(\C)$;
\item $\C^{\rho_1}\cong\C^{\rho_2}$;
\item $\Oz[i]^{j_1}\cong\Oz[i]^{j_2}$ as unital rings.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A conjugating element of $\mathcal G$ preserves $\C$ by reconstruction, so (i) implies
(ii). If $\rho_2=\kappa\rho_1\kappa^{-1}$, then
\[
 \mathcal H_{j_2}M_\kappa\mathcal H_{j_1}^{-1}\in\mathcal G
\]
conjugates $j_1$ to $j_2$. Indeed, every automorphism of $\C$ fixes $\Z$ and sends
$i$ to $i$ or $-i$, so its coefficientwise lift preserves $\Z[i]+\Ineg$. This proves
(ii) implies (i).

A coefficient conjugacy restricts to an isomorphism of fixed fields. Conversely, an
isomorphism $F_1\to F_2$ of the fixed fields extends to $F_1(i)\to F_2(i)$ by sending
$i$ to $i$; these are both $\C$. That extension conjugates the two involutions. This
proves (ii) iff (iii). Finally, the fixed-pair description and
Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:recover-pairs} prove (iii) iff (iv).
\end{proof}

In particular, involutions with the \emph{same} coefficient restriction are conjugate
by a $\C$-linear value-fixing automorphism preserving $\Oz[i]$. If the coefficient
restriction is ordinary complex conjugation, this conjugates the involution to the
standard one.

\mergetag\ The criterion is the valued, $\Oz[i]$-preserving refinement of the plain
criterion of Section~\ref{saut:sec:realforms}, by which two involutions are conjugate
in $\Aut(\K)$ exactly when their fixed real fields are isomorphic. Three consequences
for material already in the collection:
\begin{itemize}
\item The twisted conjugations $j_\theta=P_\theta cP_\theta^{-1}$ of
\eqref{saut:eq:twistedconj} lie in $\mathcal G$ ($P_\theta$ is strong,
value-fixing, and fixes $\Z[i]$ and every support) and restrict to $c$ on $\C$, so the
