proposition predicts their conjugacy to $c$ inside $\mathcal G$; their norm conjugator
is $P_\theta$ itself (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:examples}).
\item The real form of Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:inequivalentrealform} has a countable
cofinal subset, whereas every set of elements of $\No_F$ is bounded above by some
$t^{-\delta}$. So its involution is not conjugate, even by a plain field automorphism,
to any member of $\mathcal G$.
\item An involution of $\K$ that agrees with $c$ on $\C$ and lies in $\mathcal G$ is
conjugate to $c$. The forcing-sensitive involutions of
\repo{foundations-and-computation/surreal-fields-across-universes} that agree with
$c$ on $\C$ (\lbl{univ:cor:agreement}) are not conjugate to $c$; hence none of them is
both valued and $\Oz[i]$-preserving. The same holds for the proper-class family of
pure-field involutions that the report \repo{surcomplex/first-kappa-coefficients}
receives in batch~32 (\lbl{fkc:sb:thm:involutions}): they agree with $c$ on
$\C$ and none is conjugate to $c$.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Which coefficient fields occur?}

\begin{proposition}\label{saut:fs:prop:which-F}
A set-sized field occurs, up to isomorphism, as $\C^\rho$ for a nonidentity
involution $\rho$ of $\C$ if and only if it is real closed and has transcendence
degree $\cc$ over $\Q$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Necessity of real closedness is Artin--Schreier. Since $\C/F$ is algebraic,
$\trdeg_\Q F=\trdeg_\Q\C=\cc$. The last equality follows from the standard
classification of algebraically closed fields, or directly from cardinality: the
algebraic closure of a field generated over the countable field $\Q$ by an infinite
set $X$ has cardinal $\max(\aleph_0,|X|)$.

Conversely, a real closed $F$ of transcendence degree $\cc$ has algebraically closed
$F(i)$ of characteristic zero and transcendence degree $\cc$. Choosing a
transcendence-basis bijection and extending across algebraic closures gives an
isomorphism $\psi:F(i)\to\C$. Transport conjugation on $F(i)$ through $\psi$. Its
fixed field is $\psi(F)\cong F$.
\end{proof}

The standard algebraically closed field classification used here is purely
set-sized; see, for example, \cite{Lang}. The choices of coefficient isomorphisms do
not enter the explicit norm construction once an action is given.

\begin{corollary}\label{saut:fs:cor:finite-Gaussian}
Every nontrivial finite subgroup of $\mathcal G$ has order two. The kernel of
restriction $\mathcal G\to\Aut(\C)$ has no nontrivial torsion.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
\mergetag\ Both assertions are contained in Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:torsion}, which
proves them for all of $\Aut(\K)$ and for $\Aut(\K/\C)$. The source's route: restriction is
faithful on any finite subgroup by descent. A nontrivial finite group of
automorphisms of the algebraically closed set field $\C$ has order two by
Artin--Schreier and Artin's fixed-field theorem \cite[Section~4]{Conrad}. The second
assertion follows from Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:torsion}.
\end{proof}

The order-two restriction is a classical algebraic phenomenon, not a new claim
specific to surcomplex numbers. The new work is the simultaneous support-preserving
conjugation and the resulting fixed-ring classification.

\section{Integer parts, missing cuts, and the standard surreal field}\label{saut:fs:sec:integerparts}

Let $F$ be a set-sized real closed field. Give $\No_F=F((t^{\No}))$ its
leading-coefficient order and put $R_F=\Z+\Ineg_F$.

\subsection{Discreteness is weaker than the integer-part property}

\begin{definition}\label{saut:fs:def:integerpart}
A subring $R$ of an ordered field $L$ is an integer part if it is discretely ordered
with least positive element $1$ and for every $x\in L$ there is an $r\in R$
satisfying $r\le x<r+1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}\label{saut:fs:lem:discrete}
The ring $R_F$ is discretely ordered with least positive element $1$. Every nonzero
$p\in\Ineg_F$ has absolute value greater than every positive element of $F$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The leading exponent of a nonzero $p\in\Ineg_F$ is negative. Therefore its absolute
value dominates every constant, independently of whether the coefficient field
itself is Archimedean. A positive element $n+p$ of $R_F$ is consequently either an
ordinary positive integer or is larger than all ordinary integers. No element lies
strictly between $0$ and $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact integer-part criterion]\label{saut:fs:thm:ip}
The ring $R_F$ is an integer part of $\No_F$ if and only if $F$ is Archimedean. In
that case write $x=p+a+\varepsilon$ according to \eqref{saut:fs:eq:three-parts}, and
let $n\in\Z$ satisfy $n\le a<n+1$ in $F$. Its unique $R_F$-floor is
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:floor}
 \lfloor x\rfloor_{R_F}=
 \begin{cases}
 p+n-1,&a=n\text{ and }\varepsilon<0,\\
 p+n,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $F$ is Archimedean, every element of $F$ lies between consecutive ordinary
integers. A nonzero constant of $F$ dominates every infinitesimal in $\mm_F$. Thus
adding $\varepsilon$ cannot cross an integer boundary unless $a$ is already that
integer, and in that case it crosses downward exactly when $\varepsilon<0$. Formula
\eqref{saut:fs:eq:floor} follows. Discreteness gives uniqueness.

If $F$ is non-Archimedean, choose $a\in F$ with $a>n$ for every ordinary integer $n$.
No ordinary integer is within one below $a$. An $r=n+p$ with $p\ne0$ is either larger
than every constant in $F$, or its successor is smaller than every constant in $F$, by
Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:discrete}. Such an $r$ cannot satisfy $r\le a<r+1$ either.
Hence $R_F$ is not an integer part.
\end{proof}

The negative-infinitesimal correction in \eqref{saut:fs:eq:floor} is not optional. In
particular, the floor of $-t^\delta$, for $\delta>0$, is $-1$. The formula
specializes to the usual omnific floor when $F=\R$; that specialization is already
part of the collection's omnific foundations.

\subsection{When does the fixed field itself equal a surreal field?}

The order on a real closed field is definable from its field operations: a nonzero
element is positive exactly when it is a square. Every field isomorphism between real
closed fields therefore preserves order (Lemma~\ref{saut:lem:order}).

Recall the set-cut interpolation property (Appendix~\ref{saut:app:conjugacy}): for
sets $\mathcal L,\mathcal R$ in an ordered class field with every element of $\mathcal
L$ below every element of $\mathcal R$, there is an element strictly between all of
$\mathcal L$ and all of $\mathcal R$. The surreal field has this property by its cut
construction \cite{Conway}. Explicit omitted cuts are used below rather than a broad
universality characterization.

\begin{theorem}[Standard-form criterion]\label{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion}
For set-sized real closed $F$,
\[
 F((t^{\No}))\cong\No\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad F\cong\R.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
An isomorphism $F\cong\R$ extends coefficientwise and proves the reverse
implication. For the forward implication, first suppose that $F$ is
non-Archimedean. Let $F_\infty\subseteq F$ be the set of positive elements larger
than every ordinary integer. Consider the set cut
\[
 \N<F_\infty.
\]
No $x\in\No_F$ realizes it. A realizing $x$ would be positive. If $v(x)<0$, then $x$
is larger than every positive coefficient constant, contrary to $x<F_\infty$. If
$v(x)>0$, then $x<1$. If $v(x)=0$, write $x=a+\varepsilon$ with $a\in F_{>0}$ and
$\varepsilon\in\mm_F$. When $a$ is bounded by an ordinary integer, so is $x$.
Otherwise $a\in F_\infty$, and $a/2\in F_\infty$ while $x>a/2$, again a
contradiction. Thus $\No_F$ omits a set cut which $\No$ realizes.

Now suppose $F$ is Archimedean. Its rational-cut embedding identifies it with an
ordered subfield of $\R$. If it is proper, choose $r\in\R\setminus F$ and take the cut
\[
 \{q\in\Q:q<r\}\ <\ \{q\in\Q:q>r\}.
\]
Any realizing series must be bounded by ordinary integers, hence has no negative
support. Its constant coefficient $a\in F$ has the same rational cut as $r$: any
strict nonzero constant difference dominates its infinitesimal part. Thus $a=r$,
impossible. This is again an omitted set cut. Therefore $F$ cannot be a proper
Archimedean coefficient subfield, and $F\cong\R$ is necessary.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Standard conjugacy in the full plain field group]\label{saut:fs:cor:standard-conj}
An involution $j\in\mathcal G$ is conjugate to standard conjugation under an arbitrary
plain field automorphism of $\K$ if and only if $F=\C^{j|_\C}\cong\R$. In that case it
is already conjugate to standard conjugation within $\mathcal G$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A plain conjugacy induces an isomorphism $\Fix(j)\cong\No$, which forces $F\cong\R$
by Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion}. Conversely, an isomorphism $F\cong\R$
extends to a coefficient conjugacy on $\C$; Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy}
then gives a conjugacy in $\mathcal G$.
\end{proof}

\mergetag\ Together with the conjugacy criterion
Proposition~\ref{saut:prop:conjugacycriterion}, the corollary says that for
$j\in\mathcal G$ the fixed field $\Fix(j)\cong\No_F$ has the set-cut interpolation
property exactly when $F\cong\R$; the proof of
Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion} exhibits the omitted set cut when
$F\not\cong\R$. The two criteria are consistent.

\subsection{The intrinsic residue field in the Archimedean case}

\begin{proposition}\label{saut:fs:prop:residue}
If $F$ is Archimedean, the natural valuation ring of $\No_F$, defined as the elements
bounded in absolute value by an ordinary integer, is exactly $\OO_F=F+\mm_F$. Its
residue field is $F$. Consequently, if $F,F'$ are Archimedean real closed fields and
$\No_F\cong\No_{F'}$ as plain fields, then $F\cong F'$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
A series of negative valuation dominates all ordinary integers. A series of positive
valuation is infinitesimal. A series of valuation zero has the form $a+\varepsilon$
with $a\in F$ bounded by an ordinary integer, and $\varepsilon$ infinitesimal. This
proves the ring assertion. Its infinitesimal ideal is $\mm_F$, and the constant-term
residue is $F$. A field isomorphism preserves the intrinsic order and ordinary
integers, therefore preserves the natural valuation ring and induces an isomorphism
of residue fields.
\end{proof}

For non-Archimedean $F$, the outer Hahn valuation need not be the natural valuation
of the ordered field. The proposition is deliberately not stated in that generality.
The omitted-cut proof above handles that case without this identification.

\section{Exactly \texorpdfstring{$2^{\mathfrak c}$}{2 to the continuum} types}\label{saut:fs:sec:count}

\subsection{A large family of Archimedean coefficient fields}

\begin{lemma}\label{saut:fs:lem:coefficient-family}
There are $2^{\cc}$ pairwise nonisomorphic Archimedean real closed fields of
transcendence degree $\cc$ over $\Q$. They may be chosen as subfields of $\R$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose a real transcendence basis $B$ of $\R/\Q$. It has cardinal $\cc$, and it may be
partitioned as $B=B_0\sqcup B_1$ with both pieces of cardinal $\cc$. For each
$Y\subseteq B_1$, let
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:FU}
 F_Y=\{x\in\R:x\text{ is algebraic over }\Q(B_0\cup Y)\}.
\end{equation}
This is the real closure inside $\R$ of the indicated ordered field: its positive
square roots and roots of odd-degree polynomials in $\R$ remain algebraic over the
base. Thus it is real closed and Archimedean, and it has transcendence degree $\cc$
because it contains $B_0$.

Algebraic independence gives $F_Y\cap B=B_0\cup Y$. Distinct $Y$ therefore give
distinct subfields. A field isomorphism between two real closed subfields of $\R$ is
order preserving and fixes $\Q$. Rational cuts uniquely determine real numbers, so
such an isomorphism is pointwise the identity. Thus two different $F_Y$ cannot be
isomorphic.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Transport to Gaussian-preserving symmetries}

Choose an isomorphism $\psi_Y:F_Y(i)\to\C$. Let $c_Y$ be conjugation on $F_Y(i)$, and
define
\[
 \rho_Y=\psi_Yc_Y\psi_Y^{-1},\qquad
 j_Y=M_{\rho_Y}\quad\text{on }\C((t^{\No})).
\]
Every $j_Y$ is strong and value-fixing. Since every automorphism of $\C$ sends $i$ to
$\pm i$, it preserves $\Z[i]+\Ineg$. Hence $j_Y\in\mathcal G$. Its fixed pair is
isomorphic to $(\No_{F_Y},R_{F_Y})$.

\begin{theorem}[Large nonconjugate family]\label{saut:fs:thm:large-family}
The family just constructed consists of $2^{\cc}$ involutions whose fixed fields are
pairwise nonisomorphic, whose fixed rings are pairwise nonisomorphic integer parts,
and which are pairwise nonconjugate even in the full plain field automorphism
collection of $\No[i]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:residue}, an isomorphism between two fixed fields
would induce $F_Y\cong F_{Y'}$, contrary to Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:coefficient-family}
when $Y\ne Y'$. The integer-part assertion is Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:ip}. The
fixed-ring nonisomorphism also follows directly from
Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:recover-pairs}. Finally, any conjugacy of involutions
induces an isomorphism of their fixed fields, so no plain field conjugacy exists
between different members.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The upper bound and the standard exception}

The set of all maps $\C\to\C$ has cardinal
\[
 \cc^{\cc}=(2^{\aleph_0})^{\cc}=2^{\cc}.
\]
Thus $\Aut(\C)$ has at most $2^{\cc}$ elements and at most that many involution
conjugacy classes. By Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy}, these classes classify
all involutions in $\mathcal G$. Combined with Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:large-family},
this proves the exact count in Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-count}. (\mergetag\ The
coefficientwise lifts $M_\rho$, one for each conjugacy class of nonidentity
involutions $\rho$ of $\C$, form a family meeting every type exactly once.)

For $Y=B_1$, the field $F_Y$ is $\R$, so this parameter gives the standard type. For
every proper $Y$, at least one element of the transcendence basis is omitted, so
$F_Y\ne\R$. Such a field is not even abstractly isomorphic to $\R$, by the
rational-cut argument. Removing $Y=B_1$ leaves $2^{\cc}$ parameters and proves the
asserted multiplicity of nonstandard types.

\begin{remark}[What is counted]\label{saut:fs:rem:counted}
The upper bound concerns the specified class $\mathcal G$ of valued
$\Oz[i]$-preserving automorphisms. It is not an upper bound for all involutions of the
plain proper-class field. The lower-bound family remains nonconjugate in that larger
collection, which is a stronger separation statement but a different assertion from
an unrestricted upper bound.
\end{remark}

\section{Explicit examples and the content of the norm formula}\label{saut:fs:sec:examples}

\subsection{A phase-character calculation}

Suppose $\rho$ is standard conjugation on $\C$ and $\chi:\Gamma\to\C^\times$ is a
character with $\chi(\gamma)\overline{\chi(\gamma)}=1$. Define
\[
 j\left(\sum_\gamma z_\gamma t^\gamma\right)
 =\sum_\gamma\overline{z_\gamma}\,\chi(\gamma)t^\gamma,
\]
that is (\mergetag), $j=D_\chi c$ in the notation of Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:decomp}. This is a
strong value-fixing involution preserving the set of negative-support series and
$\Z[i]+\Ineg$. The norm construction gives
\[
 \mathcal H_j(t^\gamma)=\chi(\gamma/2)t^\gamma .
\]
Since $\chi(\gamma/2)^2=\chi(\gamma)$, the section uses a coherent square root
supplied by the divisible exponent group, without selecting unrelated square roots of
individual complex numbers. The conjugacy identity follows from
$\overline{\chi(\gamma/2)}=\chi(\gamma/2)^{-1}$.

Phase twists themselves already occur in the collection:
Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:phase} here, and \lbl{odg:def:lem:twist} of
\repo{surreal/omnific-diophantine-geometry}. This calculation illustrates how the
general theorem normalizes them rather than claiming them as new examples.
\mergetag\ For $\chi(\gamma)=e^{2i\theta\ell(\gamma)}$ the involution is the twisted
conjugation $j_\theta=P_{2\theta}c$ of \eqref{saut:eq:twistedconj}, and
$\mathcal H_{j_\theta}(t^\gamma)=e^{i\theta\ell(\gamma)}t^\gamma$: the norm conjugator is
exactly $P_\theta$, and the descent identity $j_\theta=\mathcal H_{j_\theta}c\,\mathcal
H_{j_\theta}^{-1}$ is the definition of $j_\theta$.

\subsection{A nonlinear two-scale involution}

Take the set-sized exponent group $\Gamma=\Q\oplus_{\rm lex}\Q$, write $z=t^{(1,0)}$ and
$u=t^{(0,1)}$, and work in
\[
 K_{\mathrm{lex}}=\C((u^{\Q}))((z^{\Q}))=\C((t^{\Q\oplus_{\rm lex}\Q})).
\]
Here $u$ is small, but every positive power of $z$ is smaller than every power of $u$.
For rational $a$, binomial powers of a principal unit are understood as their unique
formal power series with constant term $1$.

Define a $\C$-linear map $\Psi$ on monomials by
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:P-example}
 \Psi(z^a u^b)=z^a u^b(1+iu)^a.
\end{equation}
It is a strong valued automorphism. One direct verification groups a series by its
outer exponent $a$: it multiplies the $a$-coefficient by the invertible series
$(1+iu)^a$, while leaving the outer support unchanged. Within each fiber,
multiplication preserves Hahn summability. The inverse uses $(1+iu)^{-a}$, so it is
strong as well.

The map preserves the negative-support part. At a negative outer exponent, all
corrections still have negative lexicographic exponent. At outer exponent zero, the
factor is $1$, so the sign of the inner exponent is unchanged. Thus $\Psi$ and
$\Psi^{-1}$ both preserve $\Ineg$.

Let $c$ conjugate coefficients and fix $z,u$. Then $j=\Psi c\Psi^{-1}$ is an
involution. Set
\[
 W(u)=\frac{1+iu}{1-iu}.
\]
A calculation gives
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:sigma-example}
 j(z^a u^b)=z^a u^bW(u)^a,\qquad j|_\C=c|_\C,
\end{equation}
where $\overline{W}=W^{-1}$. In particular,
\[
 j(z)=z+2izu-2zu^2-2izu^3+2zu^4+\cdots.
\]
The norm conjugator is not simply $\Psi$:
\begin{equation}\label{saut:fs:eq:H-example}
 \mathcal H_j(z^a u^b)=z^a u^bW(u)^{a/2}.
\end{equation}
Nevertheless,
\[
 \mathcal H_j^{-1}j\mathcal H_j=c.
\]
The difference between $\Psi$ and $\mathcal H_j$ is an automorphism commuting with
$c$. A conjugating map need not be unique even when the norm formula selects one from
the given monomial section.

\subsection{What the finite verifier checks}\label{saut:fs:sec:verifier}

The accompanying program (\texttt{code/02-finite-symmetry-descent-verify\_finite.py})
works with exact rational complex coefficients in the finite quotient
$\Q(i)[u]/(u^N)$. It checks binomial multiplicativity, inverses,
$\overline W=W^{-1}$, the involution identity, invariance of the norm section, and the
displayed conjugacy formulas for a specified finite range of rational exponents. Its
checks are exact modulo $u^N$, not floating-point approximations.

It does not verify arbitrary Hahn summability, the maximality argument, proper-class
replacement, the Baire lemma, or the cardinality theorem. Those are proof obligations
met by the written arguments, not by finite testing. \mergetag\ The recorded run at
$N=12$ passes $3{,}751$ assertions over the $23$ exponents $a/b$ with $|a|\le4$,
$1\le b\le4$. Of these, $3{,}047$ are lexicographic comparisons of exponent pairs
(the family \texttt{negative\_support\_finite\_window}), which hold by the order
itself; the $704$ others are the identity checks listed above ($529$ of them
multiplicativity of $a\mapsto W^a$). Rerunning the program for this report
reproduced the record (README).

\section{Sharp hypotheses and invalid shortcuts}\label{saut:fs:sec:boundaries}

\subsection{Without exponent divisibility, the coefficient kernel has torsion}

\begin{example}\label{saut:fs:ex:nondivisible}
On $k((t))$ in characteristic zero, the substitution $t\mapsto-t$ fixes $k$
pointwise, preserves the valuation and negative-support part, and is a strong
automorphism of order two. It cannot be conjugate to a nontrivial coefficient action
while fixing the coefficient restriction, because that restriction is the identity.
Here $\Gamma=\Z$ is not two-divisible, so $t^{\gamma/2}$ is unavailable.
\end{example}

For an action of order $n$, the proof only requires division by $n$ in the exponent
group, together with the corresponding invariant subgroup closure in the class
argument. Divisibility is the clean uniform hypothesis for all finite groups. No
necessity theorem for every individual action is claimed.

\subsection{Fullness is needed for the surjectivity step}

On the proper subfield $k(t)\subset k((t))$, the embedding $t\mapsto t+t^2$ fixes the
coefficient field, the value group, and residue field, but is not onto: $t$
satisfies $X^2+X-(t+t^2)=0$ over the image and is not a rational function of $t+t^2$.
For instance, the nonidentity involution $t\mapsto-1-t$ fixes $t+t^2$, so $t$ cannot
lie in that rational subfield.

This example is not asserted to arise from a finite norm action, and it need not
preserve the negative-support ring. Its purpose is narrower: a value-fixing immediate
self-embedding of a smaller field need not be surjective. The full Hahn maximality
hypothesis in the proof cannot be replaced by that implication without additional
work.

\subsection{Discreteness alone does not provide floors}

For non-Archimedean $F$, the ring $R_F$ still has least positive element $1$, but the
coefficient $a>\N$ in the proof of Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:ip} has no $R_F$-floor.
Calling every discretely ordered fixed ring an ``integer part'' would erase the
central distinction of the source.

\subsection{A bijective forward-strong map is not enough as a proof}

Even when a particular definition in the literature packages strongness in terms of
normal-form expansions, inverse summability must be checked under that definition.
The proof obtains constant-term preservation only \emph{after} establishing
surjectivity and equality $\mathcal H(\Ineg)=\Ineg$. It then invokes the pairing
criterion to prove inverse strongness. Reversing that logical order would be
circular.

\subsection{The coefficient action can be wild}

The involutions $\rho_Y$ constructed in Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:count} are not assumed
continuous for the usual complex topology. Their fixed fields are ordinary
Archimedean real closed fields only in their \emph{intrinsic} order; their images
inside $\C$ need not lie on the usual real axis. It would be incorrect to replace
$F=\C^\rho$ by $\R$ merely because the ambient coefficient field is named $\C$.

\section{Position relative to existing work}\label{saut:fs:sec:position}

\subsection{Classical inputs}

Conway normal forms and the identification of omnific integers supply the ambient
objects \cite{Conway,Gonshor}. Hahn support multiplication, maximality, and algebraic
closure are classical; the exact support and maximality inputs are available in
\cite{Poonen}. The Artin--Schreier theorem accounts for real closed fixed coefficient
fields and the order-two restriction \cite{Conrad}. The classification of
algebraically closed set fields by characteristic and transcendence degree is
standard \cite{Lang}.

Kuhlmann and Serra describe valued and strongly additive automorphisms of Hahn fields
and their structural decompositions \cite{KS}. Kaplan, Krapp, and Serra treat the
surreal automorphism setting \cite{KKS}; the version checked for the source is arXiv
v3, dated 23 April 2026. These provide context for the distinction between
coefficient, value, leading-term, and summation conditions. The source does not
attribute its finite-norm formula to those papers, nor infer its novelty merely
because a search did not locate it.

\subsection{The collection at the source's pin}

The source inspected the collection at \texttt{343dc2c}: this report, the
omnific-preserving and set-sized-quotient reports, the large-cardinal report, and,
through those guides, the omnific Diophantine report. It records that the collection
contains substantial prior work on surcomplex field automorphisms, real forms,
Gaussian phase twists, omnific-preserving support stabilizers, and coefficient
reconstruction. This report already states that some involutions are not conjugate to
standard conjugation (Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:inequivalentrealform}) and relates
standard conjugacy to the set-cut interpolation property
(Proposition~\ref{saut:prop:conjugacycriterion}). Those facts are not presented as
new.

The refinement requires the involution to preserve the valuation, all Hahn sums in
both directions, and $\Oz[i]$. Within this category the source supplies an explicit
simultaneous conjugator, classifies all involutions by coefficient real closed
fields, identifies their fixed rings, and exhibits $2^{\cc}$ pairwise nonisomorphic
fixed fields with genuine integer parts. The fixed-ring reconstruction in
Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:recovery} is supporting material, not a separate novelty
claim.

The collection also has work on embeddings across universes and critical-point
defects, in the report
\repo{foundations-and-computation/large-cardinal-embeddings-and-normal-forms}, which
the source cites under the title \emph{Critical-Point Defects in Surreal
Arithmetic}. The proper-class extension here uses only local closure under a finite
family of class maps; it assumes no measurable cardinal, elementary embedding of the
universe, or nontrivial critical point. These are different research directions.

\subsection{Novelty and verification boundary}

The proposed contribution is the \emph{package}: the norm-section theorem with a
complete support, surjectivity and inverse audit, its class-local version, and the
resulting Gaussian fixed-pair classification and exact multiplicity. The argument uses
familiar algebraic devices, including norms, finite-group conjugacy, and residue
invariants. It may have antecedents or formulations in a broader valuation-theoretic
language not located by the focused search. Publication priority remains unverified.

The source comparison used selected repository guides and report descriptions rather
than every proof in every report. Some material was described there as pending review
or assembly. The source does not claim a complete nonduplication audit of that
evolving collection.

No Lean code proving these theorems is delivered, and the repository was not built.
The exact finite tests are regression checks for the worked example, not a machine
proof of the main results. Independent scrutiny should focus first on diagonal
summability, the use of set-sized maximality, the coefficient-pairing criterion, and
the class-local closure.

\subsection{Relations checked against the current collection}\label{saut:fs:sec:relations}

\mergetag\ The source's pin \texttt{343dc2c} predates the parts of
\repo{surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms} on automatic strongness and summation
detection. Checked at the merge:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Printed once, credited.} Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:pairing} and
Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:ct-strong} are \lbl{opa:as:thm:detect} and
\lbl{opa:as:thm:ctiso} (the source's Baire route is a fourth route, for the weaker
all-$y$ form); Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:reconstruction} is \lbl{opa:lem:divisible},
\lbl{odg:def:thm:multiplier} and \lbl{odg:thm:fractions}, as the source says;
Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:finite-Gaussian} is contained in
Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:torsion}; the phase twists of
Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:examples} are Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:phase} and
\lbl{odg:def:lem:twist}.
\item \emph{Stale in the source.} Its strongness hypotheses are redundant
(Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}, Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:gaussianstrong},
from \lbl{opa:as:thm:ctiso} and \lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}); its first question is
answered (Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:strong}); its second question is
\lbl{opa:as:q:gaussian} (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:questions}).
\item \emph{New relative to the collection.} The norm-section conjugator, conjugacy
within $\mathcal G$ (Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy}), the integer-part
criterion for $R_F$ (Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:ip}), the criterion
$\No_F\cong\No\iff F\cong\R$ (Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion}) and the exact
count $2^{\cc}$ (Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-count}). A search of the collection at
the merge found no other report proving them; priority beyond the collection is not
certified.
\item \emph{This report.} The plain conjugacy criterion of
Section~\ref{saut:sec:realforms} and Proposition~\ref{saut:prop:conjugacycriterion}
are refined, not replaced: within $\mathcal G$, conjugacy is coefficient conjugacy, and
set-cut interpolation of $\Fix(j)$ holds exactly when $F\cong\R$
(Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:standard-conj}). The real-form paragraph of
Section~\ref{saut:sec:questions} is answered in the valued, $\Oz[i]$-preserving case
only.
\item \emph{The omnific-preserving report.} Its statement that no nonconjugacy is
claimed for automorphisms preserving $\Oz[i]$ but not conjugation concerns its copies
of $\No$ (\lbl{opa:as:cor:complexcopies}). For involutions,
Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:large-family} gives $2^{\cc}$ members of $\mathcal G$ that are
pairwise nonconjugate under every field automorphism, in particular under every
$\Oz[i]$-preserving one; its copies are not addressed.
\item \emph{Negative information, no answer.} \lbl{odg:def:q:realform} of the omnific
Diophantine report asks which natural structure on $\Oz[i]$ determines the real
omnific subring without naming conjugation or $\Oz$. Requiring an involution to
preserve the valuation, Hahn summation and $\Oz[i]$ leaves $2^{\cc}$ pairwise
nonisomorphic fixed rings $R_F$, $2^{\cc}$ of them integer parts; so the real omnific
subring is not recovered, even up to isomorphism, as the fixed ring of an involution
singled out by those structures alone. \lbl{univ:q:compatible} of
\repo{foundations-and-computation/surreal-fields-across-universes} (which transported
real forms can preserve a prescribed valuation structure) is related and not
answered; the one consequence drawn is in Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:involutions}.
\item \emph{Complementary.} The report \repo{surcomplex/first-kappa-coefficients}
receives, in the same batch, a proper-class family of pure-field involutions that agree
with $c$ on $\C$ and are not conjugate to $c$ (\lbl{fkc:sb:thm:involutions}); by
Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy} none of them lies in $\mathcal G$. The two
results are complementary: that family shows how far the pure field is from rigid,
this one what the valuation and $\Oz[i]$ force.
\end{itemize}

\section{Further research questions of source 02}\label{saut:fs:sec:questions}

The following are proposed questions motivated by the source. Unless an external
source is explicitly named, they are not represented as longstanding published open
problems.

\begin{question}[Remove strongness for finite Gaussian symmetries]\label{saut:fs:q:strong}
Must every finite-order valued automorphism of $\No[i]$ preserving $\Oz[i]$ be strong?
More modestly, does the invariant product section remain summable under weaker
hypotheses than strongness of the original action?
\end{question}
The norm formula still makes sense on individual monomials without strongness. The
precise missing step is Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:diagonal}. A proof must control the
union of supports, not just the valuations of the terms. Conversely, a counterexample
must preserve the Gaussian ring; a plain non-strong field automorphism is not
sufficient. \emph{Status (batch~32; \mergetag).} \emph{Answered}, yes, for every valued
automorphism preserving $\Oz[i]$, of finite order or not: \lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}
(Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:gaussianstrong}). The second sentence is answered in the same
sense for the general theorem: a valued action stabilizing $k$ and $\Ineg$ is strong
(Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}), so the product section is summable under
the other hypotheses alone. Actions that are not assumed valued are not addressed, and
without valuedness $v(m_\gamma)=\gamma$ is no longer available.

\paragraph{Source 02's second question: recover the outer valuation from the Gaussian ring.}
``Does an arbitrary unital automorphism of $\Oz[i]$, extended to its fraction field,
preserve the standard Hahn valuation ring? Does the answer change for involutions or
finite group actions?'' This is \lbl{opa:as:q:gaussian} of
\repo{surreal/omnific-preserving-automorphisms} (asked there by three earlier sources),
with the additional clause on involutions and finite groups; it is recorded here
rather than as a new question. The source's comment:
Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:reconstruction} recovers $\C$ and $\Ineg$, but not by
itself $\OO$. A useful first target is an intrinsic characterization of the
positive-support part in terms of the recovered ring and coefficient field. Any
proposed characterization should be tested against the coefficientwise involutions of
Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:count}. \emph{Status:} open. By
Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:gaussianstrong}, a positive answer would make every
automorphism of $\Oz[i]$ strong, and every involution of $\Oz[i]$ subject to the
classification of Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:involutions}.

\begin{question}[Nonfull and bounded-support Hahn fields]\label{saut:fs:q:nonfull}
For which strongly summation-closed subfields, Rayner fields, or cardinality-bounded
support fields does the norm conjugator remain surjective and preserve the chosen
field?
\end{question}
The finite product does not enlarge the number of original group factors, but the
inverse need not inherit the same support bound from maximality. An exact
inverse-closure criterion would extend the theorem beyond full Hahn fields and clarify
its relation to the structural setting of \cite{KS}. \emph{Related} (\mergetag):
\lbl{opa:as:prop:kappa} proves inverse strongness for constant-term-covariant
isomorphisms of support-bounded fields with a regular uncountable bound; surjectivity
of the norm conjugator there is not addressed.

\begin{question}[Partial divisibility and ramification]\label{saut:fs:q:divisibility}
For an ordered group which is not $n$-divisible, classify the obstruction to a finite
action of order $n$ admitting an invariant multiplicative section with the original
value group.
\end{question}
The map $t\mapsto-t$ on $k((t))$ shows that torsion characters can survive. A
plausible program separates the obstruction on $\Gamma/n\Gamma$ from the
principal-unit part and determines when passing to the divisible hull removes it
without destroying the support ring. \mergetag\ Source~03 asks the same, with the
same example (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:questions}).

\begin{question}[Infinite and profinite descent]\label{saut:fs:q:infinite}
For an infinite group action, which compatible finite-level norm sections can be
assembled into a single strong conjugating map?
\end{question}
This requires a genuine summability or inverse-limit theorem. Finite-level conjugacy
alone does not provide compatible choices, and no formal infinite product over a group
should be used without an explicit support condition.

\begin{question}[Additional surreal structure]\label{saut:fs:q:structure}
Which involutions in $\mathcal G$ preserve a named real exponential structure, a
specified surreal derivation, a simplicity relation, or a distinguished monomial
section? How far do these requirements reduce the $2^{\cc}$ coefficient types?
\end{question}
The present counterexamples exploit wild coefficient real forms. Naming the standard
real coefficient field pointwise, or imposing the usual coefficient conjugation,
already reduces all admissible involutions to the standard conjugacy type. Exponential
and simplicity conditions are stronger and must be investigated in their own
languages, not silently added to this field language. \emph{Related}: the simplicity
rigidity of Section~\ref{saut:sec:exponential} and \lbl{univ:q:compatible}; neither
answers the question. \mergetag\ Source~03 asks the same for derivations, exponentials
and Hahn-analytic functions (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:questions}).

\begin{question}[Classify plain fixed fields for non-Archimedean coefficients]\label{saut:fs:q:nonarch}
For non-Archimedean real closed $F,F'$ of transcendence degree $\cc$, when are
$F((t^{\No}))$ and $F'((t^{\No}))$ isomorphic as plain fields?
\end{question}
Their support-ring pairs are isomorphic exactly when $F\cong F'$, but a plain field
isomorphism need not preserve the outer valuation. The natural valuation can combine
coefficient and exponent scales. The Archimedean residue argument therefore does not
settle this question.

\begin{question}[Definability of the standard real form]\label{saut:fs:q:definability}
Which additional set-sized predicates or parameters select the standard conjugacy type
inside $(\No[i],\Oz[i])$? Can the set-cut obstruction be expressed in a natural expanded
language without naming the whole standard real axis?
\end{question}
The multiplicity theorem rules out uniqueness from the existing algebraic, valuation,
and strong-summation conditions alone. A successful definition must distinguish $\R$
from its proper Archimedean real closed subfields, not merely detect discreteness or
the existence of floors. \emph{Related}: \lbl{odg:def:q:realform}
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:relations}).

\begin{question}[Effective inversion of the norm conjugator]\label{saut:fs:q:effective}
Can $\mathcal H^{-1}$ be described by a support-controlled transfinite algorithm with
explicit complexity bounds on natural finitely presented inputs?
\end{question}
Maximality proves existence of a preimage but is not an algorithm. The rank-two
example suggests coefficientwise inversion when the action is triangular in a
specified scale decomposition. An effective theorem should specify a representation of
supports and of the original automorphisms. \emph{Status (batch~34; \mergetag).}
\emph{Partly addressed.} Source~03 gives an explicit formula,
$\mathcal H^{-1}=\bigl(\sum_{r\ge0}(-\mathcal T)^r\bigr)D_{\chi_{\mathcal H}}^{-1}$ with
$\mathcal T=D_{\chi_{\mathcal H}}^{-1}\mathcal H-\id$ \eqref{saut:gr:eq:Hinv}, resting on the
imported formal inversion of \cite{BKKS}; it gives no algorithm on a specified
representation and no complexity bound, and its own
Question~\ref{saut:gr:q:effective} on certified support bounds is open.

\begin{question}[Formal verification]\label{saut:fs:q:formal}
Can the full set-sized norm theorem be formalized using a Hahn-series library,
followed by a separate universe-indexed or class-local transfer?
\end{question}
The best first targets are the finite-product diagonal summability lemma, the
invariant section identities, the constant-term pairing criterion, and the
immediate-extension surjectivity lemma. These are clean interfaces. Cardinality and
set-cut results can be formalized after the core conjugacy theorem rather than being
mixed into its implementation. \mergetag\ Source~03 asks the same and proposes its own order
of interfaces, with formal inversion in place of maximality
(Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:questions}).

\section{Conclusion, claim audit, and non-claims of source 02}\label{saut:fs:sec:closing}

\subsection{Conclusion}

Finite symmetries of a full Hahn field preserving its coefficient field and
negative-support part admit a concrete normalization: their action on monomials can be
absorbed into a single strong change of monomial section. The invariant product
$\prod_g g(t^{\gamma/|G|})$ is the key construction; fullness and a separate
inverse-summability argument turn it into a genuine automorphism.

For Gaussian omnific integers, this gives a complete classification in the strong
valued category (\mergetag: strongness is automatic there, so this is the valued
category). The invariant is not an additional
surreal scale, but the real closed coefficient field fixed by the coefficient
involution. Archimedeanity of that field is exactly the condition for the fixed ring to
be an integer part. Being the full real field is exactly the condition for the fixed
class field to be a copy of $\No$.

These distinctions produce the central negative conclusion: even all the stated
normal-form and integer-part requirements leave $2^{\cc}$ mutually nonisomorphic real
forms. The unresolved next step is not to repeat the finite norm calculation, but to
understand which hypotheses on arbitrary Gaussian-ring automorphisms force the
summation and valuation structure needed for it. \mergetag\ Summation is now known to
follow from the valuation (\lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}); the valuation is the open part
(\lbl{opa:as:q:gaussian}).

\paragraph{High-priority review points.}
The finite-product family is indexed by $S^G$ before its diagonal is taken; this is
why no unjustified union of arbitrary supports enters the construction. The least
exponent of each $m_\gamma$ is exactly $\gamma$, so the leading coefficient of the
least input term cannot cancel with later terms. Surjectivity is proved locally in
full set-sized Hahn fields, not by manipulating a proper-class chain of balls.

The inclusion $\mathcal H(\Ineg)\subseteq\Ineg$ is insufficient by itself to preserve
the support ring in both directions. Equality uses surjectivity and the three-region
direct sum. Constant-term preservation then follows, and only then does the pairing
criterion give inverse strongness.

For the large-family theorem, the coefficient fields are Archimedean, so the outer
Hahn valuation really is the natural valuation. No such identification is used for
non-Archimedean coefficients. The exact upper bound is stated only in the strong
valued Gaussian-preserving category, that is, for $\mathcal G$.

\subsection{A formalization-oriented interface}

A set-sized formalization can be organized around six interfaces, none of which
requires a global surreal implementation at the outset.

\paragraph{Summation interface.} A Hahn family carries proofs of well-ordered union
support and point-finiteness. Required operations are subfamilies, coefficient
rescaling, finite products, and regrouping. The diagonal lemma should consume this
interface directly, rather than a topological convergence API.

\paragraph{Section interface.} A multiplicative map $m:\Gamma\to K^\times$ has value
$\gamma$ at $\gamma$, and maps every well-ordered monomial family to a summable family.
Its strong $k$-linear extension is a field embedding. This lemma is reusable
independently of group actions.

\paragraph{Maximality interface.} A full Hahn field has no proper immediate extension.
A self-embedding preserving values and coefficient residues therefore has a surjective
underlying function. It is preferable to prove this once rather than repeat
transfinite inversion arguments in every application.

\paragraph{Support interface.} The strictly negative, zero, and strictly positive
support parts form a direct sum. A bijection fixing constants and preserving values
which maps the negative part into itself maps it onto itself and preserves the zero
coefficient.

\paragraph{Inverse-summation interface.} A $k$-linear field automorphism preserving
the zero coefficient preserves Hahn summability in both directions. The pairing
criterion supplies this interface; its only analytic-looking ingredient is the
elementary Baire argument on a countable product of discrete fields.

\paragraph{Finite-action interface.} A finite group acts by strong valued maps,
stabilizing constants and negative support. Ordered finite actions on the value group
are trivial; the product section is invariant; the preceding interfaces give the
conjugacy theorem. The class-field transfer should be a separate theorem using
invariant set-sized exponent subgroups, rather than a hidden universe coercion in this
interface.

This is an implementation plan, not a claim that any of these interfaces has been
compiled in Lean for the delivery. \mergetag\ The collection's generic Hahn-series
library is \nolinkurl{Surreal/HahnSeries/}; no \texttt{saut:fs:} label has an
implementation mapping.

\subsection{What source 02 does not claim}\label{saut:fs:sec:nonclaims}

The source states its limitations where they arise, and each is kept in place. The
list collects them, with the place where each stands in
Sections~\ref{saut:fs:sec:introduction}--\ref{saut:fs:sec:closing} (or in the
delivered README and audit). Items~1--22 are the source's; items~23--27 were added in
the merge.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2.2em]
\item Not refereed: complete written proofs are supplied relative to classical inputs;
they were reviewed during preparation but not checked by an independent referee or a
proof assistant (source title page and audit).
\item No Lean formalization is delivered; the interface of the previous subsection is
a plan (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:position}, Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:formal}).
\item Publication priority and correctness are not independently certified; the
literature and repository searches were focused, not exhaustive; the norm argument has
familiar algebraic antecedents and may have formulations in a broader
valuation-theoretic language; the word ``theorem'' implies no certification
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:position}).
\item No named unrestricted published conjecture or automorphism question is claimed
settled (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:resolved}).
\item It is not proved that an arbitrary automorphism of $\Oz[i]$ preserves the
valuation or all Hahn sums (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:resolved},
Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:valuation}). The summation half is now settled for valued
automorphisms by the collection (item~23); the valuation half is open.
\item No classification of all real forms of the plain class field $\No[i]$; the
$2^{\cc}$ upper bound concerns $\mathcal G$ only, not all involutions of the plain field
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:resolved}, Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:counted}).
\item Nothing is claimed about exponential compatibility, a derivation, simplicity or
a distinguished monomial section (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:resolved},
Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:structure}).
\item No classification of infinite or profinite group actions, no conjugacy of the
entire automorphism group to a coefficient group, and no vanishing of cohomology for
infinite groups (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:group}).
\item The outer Hahn valuation is identified with the natural valuation of the ordered
field only for Archimedean coefficients; Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:residue} is not
stated in general (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:integerparts}).
\item Artin--Schreier and Artin's fixed-field theorem are applied only in the set field
$\C$; no class Galois theory is assumed (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:involutions}).
\item The reconstruction of Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:reconstruction} is prior
supporting material, reproved, not a novelty claim; the omnific Diophantine report's
material was identified through guides and not independently audited by the source
(Sections~\ref{saut:fs:sec:recovery} and~\ref{saut:fs:sec:position}).
\item No priority is attached to the pairing criterion
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:pairing}); the order-two restriction is classical
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:involutions}).
\item The finite verifier checks exact identities in $\Q(i)[u]/(u^{12})$ and a finite
window of the lexicographic support example only; it does not verify infinite Hahn
sums, the Baire lemma, maximality, class theory or cardinalities, and its success is
not a machine verification of the main theorems (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:verifier}).
\item The repository comparison was selective: selected guides and report
descriptions at the pin, read through a connector (a local clone failed), with some
responses truncated; a failed keyword search does not establish absence; the
repository was neither modified nor built (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:position}, audit).
\item Divisibility of the exponent group is sufficient, not shown necessary for each
individual action (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:boundaries}).
\item The fullness example $k(t)$ is not claimed to arise from a finite norm action or
to preserve the negative-support ring (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:boundaries}).
\item $\mathcal G$ is second-order shorthand; the count asserts a set-indexed uniform
family, not that class automorphisms form sets or a quotient group; no class-sized
transcendence basis, class Zorn lemma or large cardinal is used
(Sections~\ref{saut:fs:sec:prelim} and~\ref{saut:fs:sec:position}).
\item The norm conjugator is not claimed unique; the formula selects one
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:examples}).
\item The research questions are proposed, not longstanding published problems
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:questions}).
\item Kaplan--Krapp--Serra (arXiv v3) was checked as context only; no theorem is
presented as solving its exponential-automorphism questions. The overview
\cite{Wiki} is orientation, not a proof reference (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:position},
audit).
\item The delivered build helper overwrites the generated PDF and verification files
of its own package; PDF metadata and compression may differ between TeX installations;
no third-party papers or font files are included (delivered README).
\item The fixed-ring reconstruction and the phase twists are not claimed as new
examples; nonstandard plain real forms are prior (Sections~\ref{saut:fs:sec:examples}
and~\ref{saut:fs:sec:position}).
\item \emph{(Merge.)} The redundancy of strongness
(Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}, Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:gaussianstrong})
and the answer to Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:strong} rest on \lbl{opa:as:thm:ctiso} and
\lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}; they are not results of the source.
\item \emph{(Merge.)} No \texttt{saut:fs:} label has a Lean implementation mapping.
\item \emph{(Merge.)} The consequences drawn for \lbl{odg:def:q:realform},
\lbl{univ:q:compatible}, \lbl{opa:as:q:otherrings} and the report
\repo{surcomplex/first-kappa-coefficients} are negative or partial information; none of
those questions is answered.
\item \emph{(Merge.)} The source's citations of Kuhlmann--Serra (Definition~4.0.5),
Poonen (Lemma~1, Theorem~1, Corollary~4) and Conrad (Theorem~3.1, Section~4) were not
rechecked for the merge.
\item \emph{(Merge.)} Of the verifier's $3{,}751$ assertions, $3{,}047$ are order
comparisons of exponent pairs; the identity checks number $704$
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:verifier}).
\end{enumerate}

\section{Gaussian-omnific real forms: conventions and statement map}\label{saut:gr:sec:introduction}

Sections~\ref{saut:gr:sec:introduction}--\ref{saut:gr:sec:closing} print the third
source of this report, \emph{Gaussian-Omnific Real Forms and Geometric
Symmetrization} (Appendix~\ref{saut:app:source03}). The source is an
\emph{independent re-derivation} of the main results of source~02
(Sections~\ref{saut:fs:sec:introduction}--\ref{saut:fs:sec:closing}): its pin
\texttt{110efe9} contains the placement of source~02 (commit \texttt{7d04483}) but
not the text of source~02, which was written into this report afterwards (commit
\texttt{b8bbfee}), and the source read only source~01's article and README. Its
results that source~02 already proves are printed once, in source~02's sections,
and credited below (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:map}); what is new or proved by a
different route is printed here, with the source's proofs. Text written for the
merge is marked \mergetag; the statement map, the conventions table and the
relations of Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:relations} are the merge's throughout.

Source~03 asks whether the Gaussian omnific ring $\Oz[i]$, together with the
Hahn valuation, selects a unique real form of $\K$. Its answer is the one of
Theorems~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-involutions}--\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-count}: no, but
there is a precise classification. The nonlinear transformations of infinite
series are removed by an arithmetic-preserving conjugation, and the coefficient
real form is the residual invariant; even the integer-part property leaves
$2^{\cc}$ inequivalent possibilities. The word ``geometric'' in its title refers to
the multiplicative symmetrization of the monomial section. There is no analytic
limit and no assertion about a surcomplex exponential; the half-exponent is taken
in the divisible ordered group $\No$.

and~\ref{saut:fs:sec:conventions} stay in force, with one exception: in
Sections~\ref{saut:gr:sec:inverse} and~\ref{saut:gr:sec:orbit} the coefficient
field $k$ is an \emph{arbitrary} field, of any characteristic, whereas in
Sections~\ref{saut:fs:sec:introduction}--\ref{saut:fs:sec:closing} it has
characteristic zero. The source's symbols were renamed as follows so that each
symbol keeps the meaning it already has in this report; no normalization changed.

The letters $c$ (conjugation), $\cc=2^{\aleph_0}$, $\mathcal G$, $\tau$ (a value
action), $\chi_j$ (a leading character), $B,B_0,B_1$ (a transcendence basis) and
$\K^j=\Fix(j)$ keep their meanings. The following readings are tempting and false.
\begin{itemize}
\item ``The orbit-product theorem is characteristic-free'' does not make the Gaussian
application characteristic-free: recovering $\Ineg$ as $\bigcap_nn\Oz[i]$ uses
$\Z\subseteq k$ and $\bigcap_nn\Z=0$.
\item $\mathcal T$ is an operator on series; it is neither the transcendental $T$ nor a
flow $T_s$. The flows $T_s$ of Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:example} are
$1$-automorphisms; $\mathcal H$ in general is not.
\item $\chi_j(\gamma)=\lc(j(t^\gamma))$ is the leading character of the involution;
$\chi_{\mathcal H_j}(\gamma)=\lc(m_\gamma)$ is that of its normalizer. They are related
by a half exponent (Proposition~\ref{saut:gr:prop:leading}), not equal.
\item ``Archimedean'' does not mean ``isomorphic to $\R$''
(Remark~\ref{saut:gr:rem:arch}).
\item Equal finite quotients and equal solvability of equations over $\Z$ do not mean
elementary equivalence (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:finite}).
\item $\operatorname{Cut}_\Q(\No_F)$ is an external subset of $\R$; no first-order
definition of it is asserted.
\item ``$\mathrm H^1(C_2,\mathcal N)$ is a singleton'' is shorthand for the cocycle
identity \eqref{saut:gr:eq:cocycle}; no class of all class cocycles is formed.
\end{itemize}
