\subsection{What is contributed, what is imported, and the scope}\label{saut:gr:sec:scope}

The source proposes as its contribution the orbit-product normalization with its
arithmetic inverse, followed by the real-form classification, the exact
integer-part criterion, the sharp count, and the intrinsic recognition of the
ordinary surreal form. Classical field classification and finite-quotient
consequences of a constant-term retraction are not new principles. It uses two kinds
of prior work: the collection's recovery of coefficient fields from omnific
arithmetic and its automatic strongness when the Gaussian integer part and the
valuation ring are both preserved (\lbl{opa:as:thm:detect}, \lbl{opa:as:thm:ctiso},
\lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}), which it reproves with attribution so as not to assume
the collection's claims; and the formal inversion of strongly contracting operators
of Bagayoko, Krapp, Kuhlmann, Panazzolo and Serra \cite{BKKS}, isolated in
Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:inverse}. Kuhlmann--Serra \cite{KS} and
Kaplan--Krapp--Serra \cite{KKS} supply the theory of Hahn automorphisms and the
surreal class setting. Artin--Schreier theory is used only for the ordinary field
$\C$, with a direct finite-polynomial argument for the class-sized fixed field
(Proposition~\ref{saut:gr:prop:split}).
\mergetag\ In the current collection the classification package was already
proved by source~02; what is new is listed in Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:relations}.

The valuation hypothesis is essential to the scope of the proof. The source does
\emph{not} show that every automorphism of $\Oz[i]$, extended to its fraction field,
preserves $\OO$, nor does it show this for arbitrary involutions of $\Oz[i]$; the
collection leaves that compatibility open (\lbl{opa:as:q:gaussian}). The
classification is complete within the valuation-compatible Gaussian sector, not
within the full abstract Gaussian automorphism collection. The normalization is
canonical relative to the selected Hahn monomials; it is not a parameter-free
definition of those monomials from the ring. It does not identify all real closed
fields of transcendence degree $\cc$ by a finite list of parameters; it proves that
no additional infinite-support twisting invariant survives once the coefficient real
field is specified.

\section{Summation detection and the formal-inversion input}\label{saut:gr:sec:inverse}

\subsection{Conventions recorded by source 03}

The source's Hahn conventions are those of Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:prelim}, for an
arbitrary field $k$ and ordered abelian group $\Gamma$: set-sized well-ordered
supports (only these when $\Gamma$ is a proper class), set-indexed summable families,
strongly additive and strongly $k$-linear maps, and the additive decomposition
$K=\Ineg_k\oplus k\oplus\mm_k$, on which $\ct$ is linear but not multiplicative
($\ct(t^\gamma t^{-\gamma})=1$); $\ct$ is multiplicative on $A_k$ and on every
$A_{\mathfrak o}$. The support facts for Hahn multiplication are
Lemma~\ref{saut:lem:support}; the source notes that they make products of summable
families summable, including their subfamilies, and that they are stronger than
convergence in a valuation topology and must not be replaced by topological
language. For $k=\R$, $\mathfrak o=\Z$ and $\Gamma=\No$ one obtains $\Oz$; for $k=\C$,
$\mathfrak o=\Z[i]$ one obtains $\Oz[i]$.

A valued automorphism $\phi$ induces the ordered group automorphism $\tau$ with
$v(\phi f)=\tau(vf)$. The source defines it by $\tau(\gamma)=v(\phi(t^\gamma))$:
independence of the representative holds because a ratio of two elements of equal
value is a unit of $\OO_k$, and the same construction for $\phi^{-1}$ gives
bijectivity. In the class case this avoids forming a quotient with proper-class
equivalence classes. The Hahn valuation of $F((t^\Gamma))$ agrees with the natural
valuation of the ordered field when $F$ is Archimedean, and need not otherwise; the
name \emph{Hahn valuation} is kept throughout. Set-sized Hahn fields are handled in
ZFC; for $\No$ and $\K$ the source works in NBG with global choice, as in
\cite{KKS}. Every family summed is set indexed, every image of a set under the
specified class maps is a set, and no class-indexed sum is used. A formula such as
``conjugacy classes in $\mathcal G$ are classified by a set'' means that there is a
set of coefficient data, each datum specifies a representative class map, and every
specified automorphism is conjugate to a representative with the stated uniqueness
property; the lower bounds are realized by actual set-indexed families.

\subsection{Detection and covariance: source 03's routes}\label{saut:gr:sec:detect}

Source~03 reproves the part of the collection's argument it needs. Its reconstruction
lemma is Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:reconstruction} (for $K=\C((t^\Gamma))$ and
$\mathfrak o=\Z[i]$), with one difference of route.

\begin{remark}[The multiplier step for any exponent group]\label{saut:gr:rem:multiplier}
In $K=k((t^\Gamma))$ with $\Gamma\ne0$ arbitrary, $(\Ineg_k:\Ineg_k)=A_k$ and
$A_k^\times=k^\times$. If $x$ has a nonzero coefficient at some $\delta>0$, then
$t^{-\delta}\in\Ineg_k$ and $\ct(xt^{-\delta})\ne0$, so $x t^{-\delta}\notin\Ineg_k$ and
$x$ is not a multiplier. No intermediate exponent is needed, unlike the proof of
\eqref{saut:fs:eq:multiplier}, which chooses $0<\delta<\epsilon$. Together with
$\Ineg_\C=\bigcap_nn(\Z[i]+\Ineg_\C)$, every field automorphism of a full complex Hahn
field with nonzero exponent group that stabilizes $\Z[i]+\Ineg_\C$ stabilizes
$\Ineg_\C$ and $\C$.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{A second route to the pairing criterion.} The source proves
Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:pairing}, with the identity
$\ct\bigl((\sum_ix_i)y\bigr)=\sum_i\ct(x_iy)$ (a finite sum) and uniqueness of the sum,
by the binary-witness route of \lbl{opa:as:thm:detect}, not by the Baire argument of
Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:row-finite}. If $S,T$ are well ordered, $S\cap(-T)$ is finite:
an infinite subset would give an increasing sequence in $S$ whose negatives decrease
in $T$; with $S=\bigcup_i\supp x_i$ and $T=\supp y$ this proves the forward direction
and the displayed identity. Conversely, the tests $y=t^{-\gamma}$ give point-finiteness.
If the union of supports is not well ordered, take a strictly decreasing sequence in
it; each support meets it finitely. Inductively choose a subsequence
$\gamma_0>\gamma_1>\cdots$ and distinct indices $i_r$ with $[t^{\gamma_r}]x_{i_r}\ne0$
and $[t^{\gamma_q}]x_{i_r}=0$ for $q>r$, discarding at each finite stage the finitely
many places belonging to previously chosen supports. Choose $b_r\in\{0,1\}$
successively with $\sum_{q\le r}b_q[t^{\gamma_q}]x_{i_r}\ne0$; at most one of the two
choices is forbidden, since the new coefficient is nonzero. Then
$y=\sum_rb_rt^{-\gamma_r}$ is a Hahn series with $\ct(x_{i_r}y)\ne0$ for every $r$, a
contradiction. Monomial tests prove uniqueness of the sum. The witness is countably
supported, also in the class case.

\begin{proposition}[Constant-term covariance]\label{saut:gr:prop:ctcov}
Let $\phi:k((t^\Gamma))\to k'((t^{\Gamma'}))$ be a field isomorphism between full Hahn
fields (exponent groups sets, or $\No$ with set supports) and $\rho:k\to k'$ a field
isomorphism with $\ct(\phi x)=\rho(\ct x)$ for every $x$. Then $\phi|_k=\rho$, and
$\phi$ and $\phi^{-1}$ are strongly additive.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $a\in k$ and every $x$, $\ct\bigl((\phi(a)-\rho(a))\phi(x)\bigr)=0$. Surjectivity
and nondegeneracy of $(x,y)\mapsto\ct(xy)$ give $\phi(a)=\rho(a)$. If $(x_i)$ is
summable and $y=\phi(w)$, then $\ct(\phi(x_i)y)=\rho(\ct(x_iw))$, nonzero for only
finitely many $i$; so the image family is summable by
Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:pairing}, and the pairing identity with nondegeneracy gives
$\phi(\sum_ix_i)=\sum_i\phi(x_i)$. The same argument applies to $\phi^{-1}$ with
$\rho^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

This is \lbl{opa:as:thm:ctiso}. The source's Theorem~3.4 (a field automorphism of
$\C((t^\Gamma))$, $\Gamma\ne0$, preserving $\Z[i]+\Ineg_\C$ and $\OO$ preserves $\C$, is
$\rho$-semilinear with $\ct\circ\phi=\rho\circ\ct$, and is strong) is
\lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}, printed here as Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}
and Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:gaussianstrong}; its proof is the three-region argument
given there.

\begin{remark}[Two circular arguments avoided]\label{saut:gr:rem:circular}
The proof does not infer strongness from continuity, and it does not assume that
$\ct$ is multiplicative on $K$. Multiplication appears only through the pairing
$\ct(xy)$, while coefficient recovery uses the smaller nonpositive-support ring.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Formal inversion (imported)}

The surjectivity of the normalizer does not follow from its leading terms alone.
Source~03 imports the following theorem.

\begin{theorem}[Formal inversion; established input]\label{saut:gr:thm:inverse}
Let $\mathcal T$ be a strongly $k$-linear map on a full Hahn series space such that
\[
 \supp\mathcal T(t^\gamma)\subseteq\{\delta:\delta>\gamma\}\qquad\text{for every }\gamma.
\]
Then the geometric operator series
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:geoinv}
 (\id+\mathcal T)^{-1}=\sum_{r\ge0}(-\mathcal T)^r
\end{equation}
is well defined and strongly linear. In particular, for every summable family
$(f_i)$ the doubly indexed family $(\mathcal T^rf_i)_{r,i}$ is summable.
\end{theorem}

For set-sized exponent sets this is, according to the source, the one-operator
specialization of \cite[Theorem~3.11 and Proposition~1.45]{BKKS}: the support
condition is their contraction condition in a totally ordered exponent set, no
uniform lower bound on the positive shifts is required, and characteristic zero is
not needed. The source imports that summability theorem rather than claiming that
strictly increasing values alone make an arbitrary geometric operator series
meaningful. The exact inverse is two-sided by the formal identity
$(1+X)\sum_{r\ge0}(-X)^r=1$ in the operator-evaluation algebra.

For $\Gamma=\No$ the source localizes. Given the union $\Omega_0$ of the supports of a
set-indexed input family, put
\[
 \Omega_{r+1}=\Omega_r\cup\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Omega_r}\supp\mathcal T(t^\gamma),\qquad
 \Omega=\bigcup_{r<\omega}\Omega_r .
\]
The set $\Omega$ need not be well ordered. The space of all well-ordered-support
series with exponents in $\Omega$ is $\mathcal T$-stable by strongness; the set-sized
theorem applies there and gives \eqref{saut:gr:eq:geoinv} for all the specified inputs
at once, independently of enlarging $\Omega$, since the same operator series is
evaluated. This defines the inverse on the class field and proves its strongness for
every set-indexed family. (Compare the invariant closure of
Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:local}.)

\begin{corollary}[An arithmetic inverse]\label{saut:gr:cor:arithinverse}
Suppose a strongly $k$-linear map $H$ on $K=k((t^\Gamma))$ has the form
\[
 H=D_\chi(\id+\mathcal T),\qquad D_\chi(t^\gamma)=\chi(\gamma)t^\gamma,
\]
where $\chi:\Gamma\to k^\times$ is a character, $\mathcal T$ is as in
Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:inverse}, and $\mathcal T(\Ineg_k)\subseteq\Ineg_k$. Then $H$ is a
linear bijection with strong inverse $H^{-1}=\bigl(\sum_r(-\mathcal T)^r\bigr)D_\chi^{-1}$,
and $H^{\pm1}(\Ineg_k)\subseteq\Ineg_k$. If $H$ is also a unital ring homomorphism and
fixes $k$, it is a field automorphism preserving every $A_{\mathfrak o}$ setwise.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
$D_\chi$ has the coefficientwise inverse $D_{\chi^{-1}}$ and preserves supports.
Every term $\mathcal T^rf$ has negative support when $f\in\Ineg_k$, and so does the
summable sum; thus \eqref{saut:gr:eq:geoinv} preserves $\Ineg_k$, and so does $H$ by its
factorization. A bijective unital field homomorphism is a field automorphism; fixing
$k$ preserves any chosen constant subring $\mathfrak o$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Relation to the leading-term kernel; \mergetag]\label{saut:gr:rem:kernel}
If $H$ is a value-fixing field automorphism fixing $k$ and
$\chi(\gamma)=\lc H(t^\gamma)$, then $D_\chi^{-1}H=\id+\mathcal T$ fixes every
leading term; for $\K$ this is the case $\rho=\id$, $\tau=\id$ of the four-layer
factorization \eqref{saut:eq:factor}, with the $1$-automorphism written to the right
of $D_\chi$ instead of to the left. Section~\ref{saut:sec:kernels} notes that
an arbitrary homomorphism $g\mapsto u_g\in1+\mm$ does not by itself give a summable
or surjective map. Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:inverse} supplies the surjectivity half,
with a strong inverse, once the forward map $x\mapsto\sum_gx_gt^gu_g$ is known to be
strongly $\C$-linear; forward summability and multiplicativity still have to be
checked.
\end{remark}

\section{Orbit-product descent in any characteristic}\label{saut:gr:sec:orbit}

The involution formula is part of a simultaneous normalization theorem, useful
independently of surreal numbers. In this section $k$ is an arbitrary field.

\begin{theorem}[Arithmetic orbit-product descent]\label{saut:gr:thm:orbit}
Let $k$ be a field, let $\Gamma$ be an ordered abelian group divisible by the positive
integer $n$ (a set, or $\No$ with set supports), and let $G$ be a finite group of order
$n$ acting on $K=k((t^\Gamma))$. Assume each $g\in G$ is valued, strongly additive and
carries $k$ onto itself; write $\rho_g=g|_k$. Put
$m_\gamma=\prod_{g\in G}g(t^{\gamma/n})$ and define $\mathcal H$ by
\eqref{saut:fs:eq:main-norm}. Then $\mathcal H$ is a strongly $k$-linear, value-fixing
field automorphism with strong inverse, and
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:intertwine}
 g\mathcal H=\mathcal HM_{\rho_g}\qquad(g\in G).
\end{equation}
If each $g$ also preserves $\Ineg_k$, then $\mathcal H$ and $\mathcal H^{-1}$ preserve
$\Ineg_k$ and every $A_{\mathfrak o}$ setwise.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Step 1: the value actions are trivial.} An increasing bijection of a totally
ordered set has no nontrivial finite order: if $\tau(x)>x$ its iterates strictly
increase, and if $\tau(x)<x$ they strictly decrease. So every $g$ fixes values.
Division by $n$ is unique because an ordered group is torsion-free.

\emph{Step 2: multiplicativity on monomials.} From $(\gamma+\delta)/n=\gamma/n+\delta/n$,
$m_{\gamma+\delta}=m_\gamma m_\delta$ and $m_0=1$. Each of the $n$ factors of $m_\gamma$
has value $\gamma/n$, so $v(m_\gamma)=\gamma$. Put $\chi_{\mathcal H}(\gamma)=\lc(m_\gamma)$;
multiplicativity of leading coefficients makes it a character.

\emph{Step 3: the sum is strong.} Let $(f_i)$ be summable and index all nonzero terms by
pairs $(i,\gamma)$. The family $(t^{\gamma/n})_{i,\gamma}$ is summable: division by $n$
preserves order, and repetition of an exponent is finite. For every $g$ the image
family $(g(t^{\gamma/n}))_{i,\gamma}$ is summable, the product family of these $n$
families is summable by the Hahn product support lemma, and its diagonal subfamily
is $(m_\gamma)_{i,\gamma}$. Multiplying terms by coefficients changes no supports;
regrouping proves that \eqref{saut:fs:eq:main-norm} exists and that $\mathcal H$ is
strongly $k$-linear.

\emph{Step 4: multiplicativity.} For $f=\sum a_\gamma t^\gamma$ and $q=\sum d_\delta
t^\delta$, both $\mathcal H(fq)$ and $\mathcal H(f)\mathcal H(q)$ are the sum of the summable
family $(a_\gamma d_\delta m_{\gamma+\delta})_{\gamma,\delta}$. Constants are fixed.

\emph{Step 5: the inverse.} Set $\mathcal T=D_{\chi_{\mathcal H}}^{-1}\mathcal H-\id$. On
each monomial the leading coefficient of $D_{\chi_{\mathcal H}}^{-1}m_\gamma$ is $1$ and
all other exponents exceed $\gamma$, so $\mathcal T$ satisfies
Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:inverse}, and
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:Hinv}
 \mathcal H^{-1}=\Bigl(\sum_{r\ge0}(-\mathcal T)^r\Bigr)D_{\chi_{\mathcal H}}^{-1}
\end{equation}
is a strong inverse; $\mathcal H$ is onto. It fixes values, as its leading terms show.

\emph{Step 6: arithmetic.} Assume $g(\Ineg_k)=\Ineg_k$ for all $g$. If $\gamma<0$, then
$t^{\gamma/n}\in\Ineg_k$, every factor of $m_\gamma$ is in $\Ineg_k$, and so is their
product; strongness gives $\mathcal H(\Ineg_k)\subseteq\Ineg_k$, hence
$\mathcal T(\Ineg_k)\subseteq\Ineg_k$. By Corollary~\ref{saut:gr:cor:arithinverse} the
inverse preserves $\Ineg_k$, and fixing $k$ gives preservation of every
$A_{\mathfrak o}$.

\emph{Step 7: intertwining.} Left multiplication by $h\in G$ permutes the factors of
$m_\gamma$, so $h(m_\gamma)=m_\gamma$; strongness and semilinearity give
$h\mathcal H(\sum a_\gamma t^\gamma)=\sum\rho_h(a_\gamma)m_\gamma$, which is
\eqref{saut:gr:eq:intertwine}. All support families are sets; the class case uses only
the localization after Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:inverse}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Two routes to one normalizer; \mergetag]\label{saut:gr:rem:routes}
The map $\mathcal H$ is the norm conjugator of Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-descent};
Steps~1--4 and~7 are Lemmas~\ref{saut:fs:lem:section}, \ref{saut:fs:lem:diagonal},
\ref{saut:fs:lem:embedding} and the intertwining of Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:norm},
found independently. The hypotheses differ. Source~02 assumes characteristic zero and
$\Ineg$-stability, and strongness then follows
(Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}); source~03 assumes strongness and neither
of the other two. The routes differ in Step~5: source~02 proves surjectivity by
spherical completeness (Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:immediate}) and local exponent closure
(Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:local}), and inverse strongness by the pairing criterion after
proving $\mathcal H(\Ineg)=\Ineg$ and $\ct\circ\mathcal H=\ct$; source~03 obtains both at
once from formal inversion, with the explicit inverse \eqref{saut:gr:eq:Hinv}. Without
$\Ineg$-stability, source~02's surjectivity argument still applies (it uses only
strongness, valuedness and $k$-stability), but its inverse-strongness argument does
not, because $\ct\circ\mathcal H=\ct$ is then unavailable; there
Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:inverse} is the only route printed here. We found no use of
characteristic zero in the proof of Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:norm} either; the
hypothesis there is needed for the Gaussian reconstruction, not for the descent.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Faithfulness on coefficients]\label{saut:gr:cor:faithful}
Under Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:orbit}, an element of $G$ fixing $k$ acts identically on
$K$; restriction to $k$ is injective on $G$. A finite-order, valued, strongly
$k$-linear automorphism of $K$ is the identity whenever $\Gamma$ is divisible by its
order.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{saut:gr:eq:intertwine} and invertibility give $g=\mathcal HM_{\rho_g}
\mathcal H^{-1}$. If $\rho_g=\id$, then $g=\id$.
\end{proof}

\mergetag\ For $K=\K$ and $k=\C$ the last sentence is contained in
Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:torsion}, which needs no valuation or strongness. For general
$k$ and $\Gamma$ it extends Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:torsion} to strong actions not
assumed to stabilize $\Ineg_k$, and to every characteristic.

\begin{remark}[An orbit product, not an additive average]\label{saut:gr:rem:norm}
The construction divides the exponent by $|G|$ before taking a norm-like product. It
does not average field automorphisms additively, which is generally not
multiplicative, and it does not select roots of unrelated elements; coherence comes
from the single monomial section and the homomorphism $\gamma\mapsto\gamma/n$. The
orbit-product theorem is characteristic-free: the inverse uses the geometric series,
not a logarithm with rational denominators. The Gaussian application takes place in
characteristic zero.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[A finite-action descent principle]\label{saut:gr:cor:fixed}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:orbit},
$K^G=\mathcal H\bigl(k^{\rho(G)}((t^\Gamma))\bigr)$. If $G$ preserves $\Ineg_k$ and
$\mathfrak o$ is a constant subring stable under the coefficient action, then
$(A_{\mathfrak o})^G=\mathcal H\bigl(\mathfrak o^{\rho(G)}+\Ineg_{k^{\rho(G)}}\bigr)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A series fixed by every coefficientwise map has all coefficients in $k^{\rho(G)}$.
Transport by $\mathcal H$ and use its arithmetic preservation.
\end{proof}

Under source~02's hypotheses this is Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:fixed-general}.

\section{Gaussian involutions: leading character and explicit inverse}\label{saut:gr:sec:gaussian}

Let $j\in\mathcal G$ be an involution and $\rho=j|_\C$. Source~03 proves
Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-involutions} by the route: $j$ is strong, semilinear and
$\Ineg$-preserving (Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:autostrong}, from
\lbl{opa:as:thm:gaussian}); its value action is an increasing involution, hence the
identity; Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:orbit} with $G=\{\id,j\}$ gives the normalizer; if
$\rho=\id$ the intertwining gives $j=\id$; and a nonidentity involution of $\C$ has
real closed fixed field and sends $i$ to $-i$ \cite[Theorem~3.1]{Conrad}. For $j=\id$
the formula gives $\mathcal H_j=\id$. The same arguments apply to a full set-sized
complex Hahn field with a nonzero divisible exponent group, for automorphisms of the
ambient field rather than merely of its support ring.

\begin{proposition}[The leading character and a coherent Hilbert~90 solution]\label{saut:gr:prop:leading}
Let $j\in\mathcal G$ be an involution, $\rho=j|_\C$, and
$\chi_j(\gamma)=\lc\bigl(j(t^\gamma)\bigr)$. Then $\chi_j:\No\to\C^\times$ is a character
with $\chi_j(\gamma)\rho(\chi_j(\gamma))=1$. The normalizer $\mathcal H_j$ has leading
character
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:bhalf}
 \chi_{\mathcal H_j}(\gamma)=\chi_j(\gamma/2),\qquad
 \frac{\chi_{\mathcal H_j}(\gamma)}{\rho\bigl(\chi_{\mathcal H_j}(\gamma)\bigr)}=\chi_j(\gamma).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Since $j$ fixes values, leading coefficients multiply, so $\chi_j$ is a character.
Write $j(t^\gamma)=\chi_j(\gamma)t^\gamma+w$ with $v(w)>\gamma$. Applying the
$\rho$-semilinear, value-fixing $j$ gives
$t^\gamma=\rho(\chi_j(\gamma))j(t^\gamma)+j(w)$, and comparing leading coefficients gives
$\rho(\chi_j(\gamma))\chi_j(\gamma)=1$. The leading coefficient of
$m_\gamma=t^{\gamma/2}j(t^{\gamma/2})$ is $\chi_j(\gamma/2)$, and
$\rho(\chi_j(\gamma/2))=\chi_j(\gamma/2)^{-1}$ gives the quotient
$\chi_j(\gamma/2)^2=\chi_j(\gamma)$.
\end{proof}

Thus the multiplicative Hilbert~90 equation for characters has a coherent
half-exponent solution, without choosing square roots separately. \mergetag\ In the
four-layer factorization \eqref{saut:eq:factor}, $\chi_j$ is the character layer of $j$
and $\chi_{\mathcal H_j}$ that of $\mathcal H_j$; \eqref{saut:gr:eq:bhalf} says that the
character layer of $j$ is the $\rho$-coboundary of that of its normalizer. The
phase-character calculation of Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:examples} is the case in which
$j=D_\chi c$ has no $1$-automorphism layer.

The full inverse is not only the diagonal correction. Define
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:Tj}
 \mathcal T_j=D_{\chi_{\mathcal H_j}}^{-1}\mathcal H_j-\id .
\end{equation}
Then, by \eqref{saut:gr:eq:Hinv},
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:Hjinv}
 \mathcal H_j^{-1}=\Bigl(\sum_{r\ge0}(-\mathcal T_j)^r\Bigr)D_{\chi_{\mathcal H_j}}^{-1},
\end{equation}
and all iterates $\mathcal T_j^r$ preserve $\Ineg$. This is where the proof keeps the
Gaussian omnific arithmetic in the inverse, rather than only producing an embedding
into the desired real form. \mergetag\ Source~02 obtains $\mathcal H^{-1}$ from
maximality, which proves existence of a preimage without a formula;
\eqref{saut:gr:eq:Hinv} and \eqref{saut:gr:eq:Hjinv} are explicit formulas, but they come
with no complexity bound, so Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:effective} is only partly
addressed (status note there).

\paragraph{No further twisting at fixed coefficient action.} If $j_1,j_2\in\mathcal G$ are
nonidentity involutions with the same restriction to $\C$, then
$\mathcal H_{j_2}\mathcal H_{j_1}^{-1}$ conjugates $j_1$ to $j_2$, fixes $\C$ and values
pointwise and preserves $\Oz[i]$: both are conjugate to the same coefficientwise map.
This is the paragraph after Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy}.

\paragraph{A nonabelian descent reading.} Let $\mathcal N$ be as in
Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:group} with $k=\C$, and let a coefficient involution $M_\rho$
act on it by conjugation. An element $\xi\in\mathcal N$ with
$\xi\,M_\rho\xi M_\rho^{-1}=\id$ gives an involution $j=\xi M_\rho\in\mathcal G$, and the
normalization supplies $\mathcal H\in\mathcal N$ with
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:cocycle}
 \xi=\mathcal H\bigl(M_\rho\mathcal HM_\rho^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}.
\end{equation}
In customary notation $\mathrm H^1(C_2,\mathcal N)$ is a singleton; in the class case
this means the concrete identity \eqref{saut:gr:eq:cocycle}. It is a Hilbert~90-type
reading of the normalizer, not a claim to originate nonabelian descent or its notation;
the general finite case is at the end of Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:group}.

\section{Classification, fixed pairs and integer parts: source 03's route}\label{saut:gr:sec:classify}

Source~03's Lemma~7.1 and Theorem~7.3 are Propositions~\ref{saut:fs:prop:which-F}
and~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy}, with the same proofs: nonidentity involutions of $\C$
up to conjugacy correspond to real closed fields of transcendence degree $\cc$; within
$\mathcal G$, conjugacy is conjugacy of the coefficient involutions, and every such field
occurs, so there is no additional character or infinite-support invariant. Its
standard inputs are the existence and uniqueness of algebraic closures and
transcendence bases \cite{StacksClosure,StacksTranscendence}.

\begin{remark}[Cardinality is not substituted for transcendence degree]\label{saut:gr:rem:trdeg}
For an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, uncountable cardinality
equals transcendence degree, since an algebraic extension of $\Q(X)$ has cardinality
$\max(\aleph_0,\abs X)$; in particular $\trdeg_\Q\C=\cc$. Equal cardinalities alone would
not classify arbitrary fields; algebraic closedness is indispensable.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Classification with coefficients fixed]\label{saut:gr:cor:fixedcoeff}
Under conjugation by elements of $\mathcal G$ fixing $\C$ pointwise, two nonidentity
involutions in $\mathcal G$ are conjugate exactly when their restrictions to $\C$ are
identical. No change of value-group coordinates is needed for sufficiency.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Necessity follows by restricting the conjugacy identity to $\C$; sufficiency is the
paragraph after Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy}.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Why a real-axis hypothesis would hide the answer.} If $j|_\C$ is ordinary
conjugation, $j=\mathcal H_jc\mathcal H_j^{-1}$ and its real form is $\mathcal H_j(\No)$.
These include real forms moved by nonlinear phase shears, but all lie in the standard
coefficient class. A nonstandard involution of the ordinary field $\C$, by contrast,
survives as an invariant that no conjugation in $\mathcal G$ removes. The main
distinction is therefore not between visible and infinitesimal motion of monomials,
but between different ordered coefficient fields.

\begin{proposition}[Real closedness directly, and the Gaussian splitting]\label{saut:gr:prop:split}
Let $j\in\mathcal G$ be a nonidentity involution. Then $\Fix(j)$ is real closed,
$\K=\Fix(j)(i)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:gaussiansplit}
 \Oz[i]=\Oz[i]^j\oplus i\,\Oz[i]^j,\qquad \Oz[i]^j=\Oz[i]\cap\Fix(j).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The fixed-pair identification \eqref{saut:fs:eq:main-fixed-pair} comes from transporting
the coefficientwise fixed pair by $\mathcal H_j$. Real closedness can be proved
directly, without applying a set-field theorem to a proper-class extension. Since
$j(i)=-i$, every $z\in\K$ decomposes uniquely as $z=a+ib$ with
$a=(z+jz)/2$ and $b=(z-jz)/(2i)$ in $\Fix(j)$; so $i\notin\Fix(j)$ and $\K=\Fix(j)(i)$.
The field $\K$ is algebraically closed. For $a,b\in\Fix(j)$ take a square root $c+id$
of $a+ib$ with $c,d\in\Fix(j)$; then $a^2+b^2=(c^2+d^2)^2$, so sums of squares in
$\Fix(j)$ are squares. A nonzero $a\in\Fix(j)$ has a square root $z\in\K$, and
$jz=\pm z$; so $a$ or $-a$ is a square in $\Fix(j)$, and not both, since $i\notin\Fix(j)$.
Thus the nonzero squares define an order. An odd-degree polynomial over $\Fix(j)$ splits
in $\K$, and $j$ pairs its nonfixed roots with their multiplicities, so it has a fixed
root. These are the usual criteria for real closedness; each assertion involves only
finitely many coefficients or roots.

If $z\in\Oz[i]$, its Gaussian integer constant term splits by the same formulas into
ordinary integers, since $\rho(i)=-i$; its purely infinite part splits into two
members of $\Ineg$, because $\Ineg$ is a $\C$-vector space and is $j$-stable. Hence
$a,b\in\Oz[i]^j$, which proves \eqref{saut:gr:eq:gaussiansplit}.
\end{proof}

\mergetag\ The first two assertions also follow from Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:torsion},
for every involution of $\K$; the splitting \eqref{saut:gr:eq:gaussiansplit} of the ring
is not stated elsewhere in the report.

\begin{corollary}[Exact integer-part criterion, three forms]\label{saut:gr:cor:ip}
For a set-sized real closed field $F$ the following are equivalent:
\textup{(i)}~$R_F$ is an integer part of $\No_F$; \textup{(ii)}~$\Z$ is an integer part
of $F$; \textup{(iii)}~$F$ is Archimedean. The floor is then \eqref{saut:fs:eq:floor}.
For a nonidentity involution $j\in\mathcal G$ with Archimedean $F=\C^{j|_\C}$, the floor
in $\Oz[i]^j$ is obtained by transporting \eqref{saut:fs:eq:floor} through the
isomorphism of pairs induced by $\mathcal H_j$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Assume (i) and apply the floor property to a coefficient $a\in F$. If a proposed floor
$p+n$ has $p\ne0$, then $a-(p+n)$ has a nonzero negative-exponent part and cannot lie
in $[0,1)$; hence $p=0$, which proves (ii). Condition (ii) bounds every coefficient
between two ordinary integers and implies (iii). Conversely, in an Archimedean ordered
field each element lies in some $[-N,N]$, and among finitely many candidate integers
there is a unique $n$ with $n\le a<n+1$; so (iii) implies (ii). For (ii)$\Rightarrow$(i):
if $a>n$, the positive constants $a-n$ and $n+1-a$ dominate $\varepsilon$, so
$0<a-n+\varepsilon<1$; if $a=n$, the sign of $\varepsilon$ decides whether to subtract
one. Discreteness is Lemma~\ref{saut:fs:lem:discrete}, and uniqueness holds because two
distinct elements of $R_F$ differ by at least one in absolute value. An isomorphism of
real closed fields preserves their unique orders, so it transports the floor
inequalities.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Two distinctions]\label{saut:gr:rem:arch}
A non-Archimedean $F$ does not make $R_F$ nondiscrete: an infinite \emph{coefficient}
lies above every ordinary integer and below every positive purely infinite element,
leaving a gap in the available floors. Conversely, an Archimedean real closed field
need not be $\R$: proper real closed subfields of $\R$ can have transcendence degree
$\cc$ and cardinality $\cc$. Replacing ``Archimedean'' by ``isomorphic to $\R$'' in
Corollary~\ref{saut:gr:cor:ip} would be false and would erase the main nonuniqueness
phenomenon.
\end{remark}

Source~03's Proposition~8.6 and Corollary~8.7 are
Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:reconstruction} with $\mathfrak o=\Z$, $k=F$ and
Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:recover-pairs}: $\Frac(R_F)=\No_F$, $\Ineg_F=\bigcap_nnR_F$,
$(\Ineg_F:\Ineg_F)=F+\Ineg_F$ in $\Frac(R_F)$ with units $F^\times$, and
$R_F\cong R_{F'}$ exactly when $F\cong F'$. So the coefficient invariant is recoverable
from the \emph{abstract ring} $\Oz[i]^j$, without an a priori valuation or monomial
section. The fraction argument uses the proper-class upper-bound property of $\No$ and
must not be applied silently to a fixed set-sized Hahn field, whose supports can be
unbounded in its exponent group and whose negative-support ring can have a smaller
fraction field (Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:prelim}).

\section{Counting, rational cuts and the standard form}\label{saut:gr:sec:count}

Source~03's counting argument (its Lemmas~9.1, 9.2 and Theorem~9.3) is that of
Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:count}, with the same fields $F_Y$, the same coefficientwise
involutions $j_Y=M_{\rho_Y}$ and the same bound $\abs{\C}^{\abs{\C}}=2^{\cc}$, without
the continuum hypothesis; ordered subfields of $\R$ are rigid because they fix $\Q$ and
reals are separated by rationals, and a field isomorphism of real closed fields is an
ordered one. The isomorphism $\psi_Y:F_Y(i)\to\C$ is an abstract field isomorphism,
not the inclusion into the ordinary complex plane, which is not onto when $F_Y\ne\R$;
equal transcendence degree supplies it, and no continuity in the complex topology is
required or asserted.

For an Archimedean $F\subseteq\R$, source~03 separates the fixed fields by the
external set of realized rational cuts,
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:cuts}
 \operatorname{Cut}_\Q(\No_F)=\bigl\{r\in\R:\ \text{some }x\in\No_F\text{ has }q<x<q'
 \text{ for all }q,q'\in\Q\text{ with }q<r<q'\bigr\}.
\end{equation}

\begin{remark}[Recovered rational cuts]\label{saut:gr:rem:cuts}
For every Archimedean real closed $F\subseteq\R$, $\operatorname{Cut}_\Q(\No_F)=F$. An
element realizing a cut is bounded by ordinary integers, so it has no negative-exponent
part and is $a+\varepsilon$ with $a\in F$, $\varepsilon\in\mm_F$; the infinitesimal is
below every positive rational, so the cut is that of $a$, forcing $r=a$; conversely
$x=r$ realizes the cut of $r\in F$. This is the argument of the Archimedean case of
Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion}, and its valuation form is
Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:residue}. Any field isomorphism of real closed fields
preserves order and fixes $\Q$, hence preserves $\operatorname{Cut}_\Q$; so the fields
$\Fix(j_Y)$ are pairwise nonisomorphic, the $j_Y$ are pairwise nonconjugate under
arbitrary field automorphisms of $\K$, and none except the class with $F_Y\cong\R$ has
fixed field isomorphic to $\No$, whose realized cuts are all of $\R$
(Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:large-family}). No first-order definability of this external
invariant is asserted.
\end{remark}

Every set of elements of $\No_F$, for any real closed $F$, has an upper bound
$t^{-\delta}$: choose a positive surreal $\delta$ with $-\delta<v(x)$ for all nonzero
$x$ in the set. So every fixed field in the valued Gaussian sector has no set-sized
cofinal subset, and the countably cofinal real form of
Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:inequivalentrealform} has no involution preserving both $\Oz[i]$
and $\OO$ (second consequence after Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy}). The
nonstandard Archimedean examples thus differ from $\No$ by missing bounded rational
cuts, not by a small cofinal sequence. None of this bounds the number of conjugacy
classes of all involutions of the plain field: the exact upper bound belongs to the
valued Gaussian sector (Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:counted}).

\begin{corollary}[Standard arithmetic form criterion]\label{saut:gr:cor:standard}
Let $j\in\mathcal G$ be a nonidentity involution and $F=\C^{j|_\C}$. The following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $j$ is conjugate to $c$ by an element of $\mathcal G$;
\item $F\cong\R$ as a field;
\item $\Oz[i]^j\cong\Oz$ as a ring;
\item $(\Fix(j),\Oz[i]^j)\cong(\No,\Oz)$ as a field--subring pair;
\item $\Fix(j)\cong\No$ as a plain field.
\end{enumerate}
If $\Oz[i]^j$ is an integer part of $\Fix(j)$, they are also equivalent to
\textup{(vi)}: $\Fix(j)$ realizes every rational cut arising from an ordinary real number.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
(i)$\Leftrightarrow$(ii) is Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy}. Since the fixed
ring is $\cong R_F$ and $\Oz=R_\R$, (ii)$\Leftrightarrow$(iii) is
Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:recover-pairs}. (i) implies (iv) by restricting a
conjugator, and (iv) implies (iii); alternatively (ii) gives (iv) by a coefficientwise
isomorphism followed by $\mathcal H_j$. The source proves (v) and (vi) under the
integer-part hypothesis: then $F$ is Archimedean, embed it in $\R$ by its rational-cut
embedding; by Remark~\ref{saut:gr:rem:cuts} $\Fix(j)$ realizes exactly the cuts of that
copy of $F$, all of $\R$ exactly when the copy is $\R$; a field isomorphism
$\Fix(j)\cong\No$ forces equality of realized-cut sets, and the converse follows from
(iv). \mergetag\ Without that hypothesis, (ii)$\Leftrightarrow$(v) is
Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion} (Corollary~\ref{saut:fs:cor:standard-conj}), so
(v) needs no hypothesis. Clause (vi) does need it: if $F$ is the real closure of
$\R(X)$ ordered with $X$ above every real, then $F$ is non-Archimedean of transcendence
degree $\cc$, $\No_F\supseteq\R$ realizes every real rational cut, and $\No_F\not\cong\No$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The source's caution, and its status]\label{saut:gr:rem:plain}
The source notes that for non-Archimedean $F$ the Hahn residue field and the natural
ordered residue field need not coincide, and so does not use the plain-field criterion
$\Fix(j)\cong\No$ without the integer-part hypothesis; the ring criterion (iii) holds
for every $j$ in the sector. \mergetag\ The caution is correct as a statement about its
own proof. The plain-field criterion was already proved for every set-sized real
closed $F$ by explicit omitted set cuts (Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion}); the
cut $\N<F_\infty$ of its proof avoids the residue-field comparison.
\end{remark}

The proof also explains why agreement with a familiar collection of ordinary
congruences is insufficient to recognize $\Oz$: those congruences do not see the
recovered coefficient field.

\section{What finite arithmetic cannot distinguish}\label{saut:gr:sec:finite}

The following are direct retraction arguments. For the ordinary omnific ring their
counterparts are already in the collection; the point is that they hold uniformly for
the mutually nonisomorphic descended rings of Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:large-family}.

\begin{proposition}[Uniform finite quotients]\label{saut:gr:prop:finite}
For every real closed field $F$ and every positive ordinary integer $n$,
\[
 nR_F=n\Z\oplus\Ineg_F,\qquad R_F/nR_F\cong\Z/n\Z .
\]
Every unital ring homomorphism from $R_F$ to a finite ring factors through
$\ct:R_F\to\Z$. Consequently all nonzero finite quotients are the ordinary $\Z/n\Z$, and
the inverse system of ordinary congruence quotients is independent of $F$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The additive group $\Ineg_F$ is divisible by every positive ordinary integer, so the
first identities follow by taking constant terms. If a finite target ring has additive
exponent $m$, then for $p\in\Ineg_F$ write $p=mq$ with $q\in\Ineg_F$; every homomorphism
kills $p$, so it factors through $R_F/\Ineg_F=\Z$. A nonzero finite quotient is a
nonzero quotient of $\Z$ of finite cardinality.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Ordinary polynomial solvability is unchanged]\label{saut:gr:prop:dio}
A finite system of polynomial equations with coefficients in $\Z$ has a solution in
$R_F$ if and only if it has a solution in $\Z$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
$\Z$ embeds in $R_F$. Conversely, apply the ring retraction $\ct:R_F\to\Z$ to every
coordinate of a solution; polynomial evaluation commutes with ring homomorphisms.
\end{proof}

Thus $2^{\cc}$ pairwise nonisomorphic integer-part rings have identical finite quotient
systems and identical truth values for existential polynomial \emph{equation} systems
over ordinary integer coefficients. This is not an assertion of elementary equivalence,
and it does not cover disequations, order constraints, or coefficients from the
nonordinary parts of the rings. Constant extraction can kill a nonzero purely infinite
element, so a disequation cannot be transported by the same argument.

\mergetag\ For $F=\R$ these are \lbl{odg:thm:finitequotients} and \lbl{odg:thm:transfer}
of the omnific Diophantine report (the latter also for positive existential
formulas at ordinary integer parameters, and the same retraction proves that extension
here), and for the definable rings $I_A$ they are \lbl{dsn:thm:finite-quotients} of the definable-surreals report. The
factorization clause is the finite-target case of \lbl{osq:thm:universal}(ii) of the
set-sized-quotient report, applied with the set field $F$
and the subring $\Z$ (that report's $D$): every unital homomorphism from $R_F$ to any set-sized ring factors through
$\ct$. What is new here is the uniformity over the $2^{\cc}$ nonisomorphic rings, which
gives negative information on \lbl{odg:def:q:realform} beyond
Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:relations}: congruences and integer equations do not
distinguish the fixed rings.

\section{A rank-two flow example and the finite checks of source 03}\label{saut:gr:sec:example}

The classification should not be read as saying that all involutions act
coefficientwise before conjugation. Work in
$K_{\mathrm{lex}}=\C((t^{\Q\oplus_{\rm lex}\Q}))$ with $z=t^{(1,0)}$ and $u=t^{(0,1)}$ as in
Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:examples}, and define on monomials
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:D}
 \mathcal D(z^au^b)=a\,z^au^{b+1}\qquad(a,b\in\Q).
\end{equation}
The coefficient field is $\C$, on which $\mathcal D$ acts trivially; additivity of the
first exponent coordinate gives the derivation rule, translation by $(0,1)$ gives
strongness, and $\mathcal D^r(z^au^b)=a^rz^au^{b+r}$. The sums
\begin{equation}\label{saut:gr:eq:flow}
 T_s(z^au^b)=z^au^b\exp(sau)=\sum_{r\ge0}\frac{(sa)^r}{r!}z^au^{b+r}\qquad(s\in\C)
\end{equation}
define strong automorphisms with $T_sT_{s'}=T_{s+s'}$. Here $\exp$ is a formal power
series at the positive-value element $sau$, not a global surcomplex exponential. For a
summable input support $S$, all output supports lie in $S+\N(0,1)$, and the support
lemma gives the required summability. \mergetag\ These are the flows of
Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:shiftflow} in the set-sized workspace $\Q\oplus_{\rm lex}\Q$, with
the additive map $(a,b)\mapsto a$ and shift $\delta=(0,1)$; that theorem also shows they
are $1$-automorphisms.

\begin{proposition}[A phase-twisted real form]\label{saut:gr:prop:phase}
Let $c$ be coefficientwise conjugation on $K_{\mathrm{lex}}$ and $j=T_ic$. Then $j$ is an
involution preserving the valuation ring and $\Z[i]+\Ineg$, it does not preserve the
real-coefficient Hahn field $\R((t^{\Q\oplus_{\rm lex}\Q}))$ setwise, and its normalizer is
$\mathcal H_j=T_{i/2}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Since $cT_ic=T_{-i}$, $j^2=\id$. For a negative exponent $(a,b)$: if $a<0$, every
$(a,b+r)$ is negative; if $a=0$ and $b<0$, the factor $a^r$ makes the monomial fixed.
Strongness gives preservation of $\Ineg$, and the inverse has the same property;
constants in $\Z[i]$ are fixed by $T_i$ and preserved by $c$. The image
$j(z)=z\exp(iu)$ has a nonzero imaginary coefficient at the next exponent. Finally, for
$\gamma=(a,b)$,
\[
 t^{\gamma/2}j(t^{\gamma/2})=z^au^b\exp(iau/2)=T_{i/2}(t^\gamma).
\]
\end{proof}

The fixed field is $T_{i/2}\bigl(\R((t^{\Q\oplus_{\rm lex}\Q}))\bigr)$, and the fixed Gaussian
part is the transported real support ring. The example illustrates the theorem; phase
shears themselves are not claimed new, since related shears and support-cut criteria
already occur in the collection (\lbl{opa:thm:realaxis}, Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:phase}).
\mergetag\ It belongs to the same family as the example \eqref{saut:fs:eq:sigma-example}:
for $\varphi\in1+u\C[[u]]$ with $\overline\varphi=\varphi^{-1}$, the map
$z^au^b\mapsto z^au^b\varphi(u)^a$ composed with $c$ is an involution in the same class,
with normalizer $z^au^b\mapsto z^au^b\varphi(u)^{a/2}$; source~02 takes
$\varphi=W=(1+iu)/(1-iu)$ and source~03 takes $\varphi=\exp(iu)$.

\subsection{What source 03's verification program checks}\label{saut:gr:sec:verifier}

The program \texttt{code/03-gaussian-real-forms-verify.py} uses exact arithmetic in
$\Q(i)$ and finite truncations in the inner monomial $u$: its test algebra is
$\Q(i)[z^{\Q}][u]/(u^{N+1})$ with finite outer support, at order $N=8$ and seed
$20260923$. It tests the formal identities $T_sT_r=T_{s+r}$, $j^2=\id$,
$j\mathcal H_j=\mathcal H_jc$ and $\mathcal H_j(fq)=\mathcal H_j(f)\mathcal H_j(q)$, finite geometric
inverses, and the sign of sampled lexicographic support shifts. These are finite
regression checks of signs, coefficients and normalization formulas; they do not
establish universal Hahn summability, class-sized assertions, existence of
transcendence bases or cardinality claims, which rest on the proofs and the stated
inputs. No Lean source is supplied.

\mergetag\ The recorded run passes $457$ assertions: $9$ each for the orbit-product formula,
the monomial involution and the monomial intertwining (the monomials $z^a$,
$a\in\{-2,-\tfrac32,\ldots,2\}$), $24$ each for eleven identities on random series
($264$), and $166$ in the family \texttt{negative\_support\_preservation}. Those $166$ are
comparisons $(a,b+r)<(0,0)$ with $a<0$, or $a=0$, $r=0$, $b<0$, which hold by the
lexicographic order itself. The normalizer is implemented as the flow $T_{i/2}$; the
orbit-product formula $t^{\gamma/2}j(t^{\gamma/2})$ is compared with it only on the nine
monomials. In the truncated algebra the remainder $\mathcal T$ is nilpotent, so the
geometric inverse is a finite sum there (the check \texttt{nilpotent\_remainder}); the
program does not test the summability of an infinite operator series. Rerunning it for
this report reproduced the record (README).

\section{Questions of source 03}\label{saut:gr:sec:questions}

The source proposes twelve questions, in increasing distance from its proof; they are
research questions, not assertions that each is a previously published open problem.
\mergetag\ Six coincide with questions already in the collection and are recorded
as paragraphs that cite those questions' labels; one is printed with its answer, which
was already in the report; five are new.

\paragraph{Its Questions~1 and~2: automatic valuation preservation.}
``Does every unital automorphism of $\Oz[i]$, extended to $\Frac(\Oz[i])=\K$, preserve
the Hahn valuation ring?'' and, even if not, ``must an automorphism of order two of
$\Oz[i]$ have a valuation-compatible extension?'' These are \lbl{opa:as:q:gaussian} and
the involution clause of source~02's second question
(Section~\ref{saut:fs:sec:questions}). The source's comments: an affirmative answer
would turn the classification into one of all abstract Gaussian omnific involutions;
the multiplier construction recovers $\C$ and $\Ineg$, and what is missing is recovery
of the complementary positive-support part without an order. Finite order might rule
out behaviour available to arbitrary maps, but the orbit-product formula cannot be used
before strongness is proved, since its diagonal summability would be unavailable; a
useful intermediate goal is to recover constant extraction for finite-order maps.
\emph{Status:} open. \mergetag\ With the valuation, strongness is automatic
(Remark~\ref{saut:fs:rem:gaussianstrong}), so for involutions the missing step is
exactly the valuation.

\begin{question}[Nonvalued arithmetic real forms]\label{saut:gr:q:nonvalued}
Can a real form occur whose involution preserves $\Oz[i]$ but not its Hahn valuation? If
so, what invariant replaces the coefficient-field classification, and can there be more
than $2^{\cc}$ conjugacy classes?
\end{question}
The bound of Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-count} holds in the valued sector because every
action there has a coefficientwise representative; it gives no bound once that
hypothesis is removed. A counterexample would have to explain exactly how the intrinsic
negative-support and coefficient substructures fail to determine the valuation.
\mergetag\ A negative answer to the first clause is the involution case of
\lbl{opa:as:q:gaussian}. The proper-class family of \lbl{fkc:sb:thm:involutions}
(Appendix~\ref{saut:app:conjugacy}) lies outside $\mathcal G$; whether any of its members
preserves $\Oz[i]$ is not known.

\paragraph{Its Question~4: removing exponent divisibility.} ``For an ordered abelian
group not divisible by the order of a finite action, what is the obstruction to
orbit-product normalization?'' This is Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:divisibility}, with the
same example $t\mapsto-t$ on $\C((t^\Z))$ (Example~\ref{saut:fs:ex:nondivisible}). The
source adds: the half-exponent cannot simply be written down; the example shows that
divisibility is a real boundary, not an artifact of notation; a classification should
separate character obstructions from genuinely nonlinear ones. \emph{Status:} open.

\paragraph{Its Question~5: general arithmetic coefficient rings.} ``For a
characteristic-zero field $k$ and a unital subring $\mathfrak o$, which hypotheses on
$\mathfrak o$ make preservation of $A_{\mathfrak o}$ recover $k$ and constant extraction?''
This is \lbl{opa:as:q:otherrings}. The source adds that the orbit-product theorem already
handles the descent once strongness and coefficient preservation are available, that a
systematic reconstruction theorem would extend the Gaussian case to other arithmetic
coefficient rings and their finite Galois actions, and that the intersection
$\bigcap_nn\mathfrak o$ must be controlled: it is zero for $\Z$ and $\Z[i]$ but not for
every coefficient ring. \emph{Status:} open.

\begin{question}[Positive characteristic]\label{saut:gr:q:charp}
Which arithmetic predicates force coefficient reconstruction and automatic strongness
in positive characteristic, especially for finite actions whose orders are divisible by
the characteristic?
\end{question}
The monomial orbit-product theorem already works in every characteristic
(Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:orbit}): it divides in the exponent group, not in the
coefficient field. Automatic strongness from integer divisibility has no direct
analogue, and wild finite-order automorphisms can appear when exponent divisibility
fails. A precise theorem must specify both the summability structure and the arithmetic
subring.

\begin{question}[Intrinsic theories of descended integer parts]\label{saut:gr:q:theories}
For Archimedean real closed fields $F,F'$ of transcendence degree $\cc$, when are $R_F$
and $R_{F'}$ elementarily equivalent as rings?
\end{question}
They have identical ordinary equation solvability and finite quotients
(Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:finite}) but need not be isomorphic. Coefficient recovery uses
an intersection over all positive integers, an external construction that is not
automatically a first-order interpretation. Locating the first-order information about
the omitted real cuts is a separate model-theoretic problem. \mergetag\ Compare
\lbl{fkc:sb:q:omnific} of \repo{surcomplex/first-kappa-coefficients}, which asks whether
the rings of short omnific integers are elementarily equivalent.

\begin{question}[Which fixed fields are abstractly surreal?]\label{saut:gr:q:abstract}
For non-Archimedean $F$ of transcendence degree $\cc$, characterize when $F((t^{\No}))$
is isomorphic as an ordered field to $\No$.
\end{question}
The source comments that the integer-part property fails for $R_F$, but the plain field
may have a natural valuation different from the Hahn valuation, so the Archimedean cut
invariant no longer suffices; one should compare natural residue fields, value groups
and realization of set-sized cuts, keeping the arithmetic pair separate from the plain
field. \emph{Status} (\mergetag): \emph{answered} before the source was merged, and
negatively: by Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion}, $F((t^{\No}))\cong\No$ (even as a
plain field) only if $F\cong\R$, so never for non-Archimedean $F$; the omitted set cut is
$\N<F_\infty$. The finer question of when two such fields are isomorphic to each other is
Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:nonarch}, open.

\begin{question}[Effective normalizers]\label{saut:gr:q:effective}
For computable finite-rank or finitely described Hahn actions, can one compute specified
coefficients of $\mathcal H$ and $\mathcal H^{-1}$ with certified bounds on the amount of
input support required?
\end{question}
The inverse \eqref{saut:gr:eq:Hinv} is a genuine strong operator series, but that does not
by itself give a termination bound in an arbitrary representation. A useful first domain
is rational lexicographic rank with finite shift rules; the program of
Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:verifier} covers only a homogeneous rank-two test case, not an
algorithm for arbitrary surreal inputs. \mergetag\ Related:
Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:effective}, which \eqref{saut:gr:eq:Hinv} addresses in part.

\paragraph{Its Question~10: compatibility with extra analytic structure.} ``Which
normalized real forms are compatible with a specified derivation, exponential, or selected
class of Hahn-analytic functions?'' This is Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:structure}. The source
adds: the conjugators preserve ring arithmetic and valuation, not the Gonshor exponential
or the Berarducci--Mantova derivation; additional structure may shrink the coefficient
possibilities dramatically; a rigidity claim must identify the additional operations and
check that the normalizer transports them rather than treating them as automatic
field-theoretic data. \emph{Status:} open.

\begin{question}[Hermitian and norm descent]\label{saut:gr:q:hermitian}
How do integral Hermitian forms, unitary groups and norm equations over $\Oz[i]$ change
as its real structure ranges over the classes of Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-count}?
\end{question}
The splitting \eqref{saut:gr:eq:gaussiansplit}, $\Oz[i]=\Oz[i]^j[i]$, and the transported
positive norm are a starting point. Ordinary finite quotient information is unchanged, so
distinctions must use coefficients, order or support geometry. This connects with the
collection's work on omnific groups and lattices
(\repo{surreal/omnific-groups-and-lattices}); no classification of those objects is
claimed.

\paragraph{Its Question~12: formal verification and foundation minimization.} ``Can the
orbit-product normalization be formalized for set-sized Hahn fields and then transported to
a universe-controlled surreal implementation with explicit smallness assumptions?'' This
is Question~\ref{saut:fs:q:formal}. The source proposes the order: the scalar detector,
strong semilinear maps, diagonal product summability, the operator-inverse input,
arithmetic preservation of the inverse, and the conjugacy identity; the counting argument
and the real closed coefficient classification can be formalized separately in ordinary
set mathematics, and a final stage should express class-map assertions without a type of
all class maps. \emph{Status:} open; no \texttt{saut:gr:} label has a Lean mapping.

\section{Position, audit and non-claims of source 03}\label{saut:gr:sec:closing}

\subsection{The source's comparison at its pin, and a stale claim}\label{saut:gr:sec:position}

The source inspected the collection at \texttt{110efe9}: the root README, the catalogue,
the omnific-preserving report with its source audit, and this report's
\texttt{article.tex} and README. It records that the omnific-preserving report proves
the valuation-compatible Gaussian automatic-strongness theorem, and that this report
discusses general real forms, finite subgroups, phase twists and nonconjugate real forms
without a Gaussian classification. It then states that, at that snapshot, the inspected
sources do not supply the orbit-product normalizer with its arithmetic inverse, the
classification, or the integer-part and cardinality package, and that its comparison is
targeted, not exhaustive over files, earlier commits, unintegrated manuscripts or
unpublished work.

\mergetag\ That statement is stale and is not printed as current. At the pin this
report's \texttt{article.tex} was source~01 alone, as the source says; but the
directory already held source~02's delivered audit (placed in \texttt{7d04483}, an
ancestor of the pin), which names the section $m_\gamma=\prod_gg(t^{\gamma/n})$, the
classification of valued Gaussian-preserving involutions, the Archimedean integer-part
criterion and the exact $2^{\cc}$ count; the source did not read that file. Source~02's
text was written into this report in \texttt{b8bbfee}, after the pin
(Sections~\ref{saut:fs:sec:introduction}--\ref{saut:fs:sec:closing}). In the current
collection the normalizer, the classification and the integer-part and cardinality
package are source~02's, and source~03 is an independent re-derivation of them. Its
inverse formula is new (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:relations}).

On novelty the source says: the proposed package rests on established Hahn support
theory, formal operator calculus, Artin--Schreier theory and classical
transcendence-basis arguments; a norm-like construction and a Hilbert~90-type vanishing
statement naturally have classical analogues, and novelty of the exact Hahn and Gaussian
formulations requires expert comparison and cannot be certified by a web search. It
announces no solution of a named published open problem; it resolves a stated
classification problem within the valued Gaussian sector, retains the broader
compatibility question as open, and treats priority and independent correctness
certification as separate questions. Of Kaplan--Krapp--Serra \cite{KKS} (arXiv v3), no
unresolved question is claimed solved by the classification.

\subsection{Conclusion of source 03}

Gaussian omnific arithmetic together with the Hahn valuation does not select one real
form. It makes finite symmetries regular enough for a multiplicative normalization of the
monomial section; all support-level twisting can then be removed, leaving the coefficient
real field as the complete conjugacy invariant. The integer-part property detects
Archimedeanness of that field, not Dedekind completeness, which is enough to produce
$2^{\cc}$ mutually inequivalent real forms with integer parts, including examples not
isomorphic to $\No$ even as plain ordered fields, while their ordinary finite quotients and
polynomial-equation solvability over $\Z$ coincide. The strongest next question is
whether Gaussian omnific arithmetic alone forces valuation compatibility, at least for
involutions; the answer would decide whether the classification covers every arithmetic
real structure or only its valued part.

\subsection{Proof dependency and hypothesis audit}

The main proof has the dependency order: $\Oz[i],\OO\Rightarrow\Ineg,\C,\mm\Rightarrow\ct
\Rightarrow$ strong semilinearity; finite action $\Rightarrow$ trivial value action;
orbit product and strongness $\Rightarrow$ strong multiplicative $\mathcal H$;
contracting remainder and formal inversion $\Rightarrow$ $\mathcal H^{-1}$ preserves $\Ineg$;
conjugacy normal form $\Rightarrow$ coefficient invariant $\Rightarrow$ integer-part
criterion and counting. No arrow uses the conclusion of a later one.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\RaggedRight\arraybackslash}p{0.3\textwidth}>{\RaggedRight\arraybackslash}p{0.62\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Hypothesis or distinction & Where it is used\\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Hypothesis or distinction & Where it is used\\
\midrule
\endhead
Full Hahn field & The scalar detector needs every countably supported test series; formal
inversion acts on full support spaces.\\
Surjectivity of the original maps & Constant-term tests in the target must have preimages;
preservation of support pieces must be onto.\\
Preservation of $\OO$ & Gives preservation of $\mm$, hence full constant-term covariance
after arithmetic reconstruction.\\
Divisibility by $\abs G$ & Makes $t^{\gamma/\abs G}$ a coherent monomial family; it cannot be
replaced by isolated root choices.\\
Strongness & Makes the diagonal orbit-product family summable and transports the conjugacy
identity from monomials to series.\\
Contracting-operator summability & Proves that the normalizer is onto; leading terms alone
give only injectivity.\\
Preservation of $\Ineg$ & Ensures that the normalizer and its geometric inverse preserve the
negative-support ring.\\
Archimedean coefficient field & Exactly the floor condition for constants; identifies the
Hahn valuation with the natural one.\\
Proper-class exponent group $\No$ & Every set support has an upper bound, needed for
$\Frac(R_F)=\No_F$.\\
Fixed coefficient field vs.\ fixed residue field & They coincide under the reconstruction,
not for arbitrary valued surcomplex automorphisms.\\
Class maps vs.\ a class of maps & Only individual maps and set-indexed representatives are
constructed.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}
The inverse is checked on both sides by the formal identity $(1+X)\sum_r(-X)^r=1$; it is not
only a pointwise solution of the intertwining equation. Preservation of $\Ineg$ by each
summand gives preservation by the sum, since all sums are Hahn sums with coefficientwise
finite contributions.

\subsection{What source 03 does not claim}\label{saut:gr:sec:nonclaims}

The source states its limitations where they arise, and each is kept in place. The list
collects them, with the place where each stands (in
Sections~\ref{saut:gr:sec:introduction}--\ref{saut:gr:sec:closing}, or in the shipped
\texttt{03-gaussian-real-forms-SOURCES.md}, the verification record, and the delivery
README, which is not shipped). Items~1--25 are the source's; items~26--31 were added in
the merge.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,leftmargin=2.2em]
\item An AI-assisted, unrefereed research draft; the proofs are intended to establish the
claims under their assumptions, but independent correctness certification is not claimed
(title page, README).
\item Novelty and priority are not certified; the literature and repository searches were
targeted, and failing to find the package is not proof of priority; independent expert
review is needed before any publication claim
(Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:position}, \texttt{SOURCES.md}).
\item Classical facts (real closed coefficient fields, Galois descent, Hilbert~90 for
characters, constant-term retractions) are not presented as new; the finite-quotient and
equation propositions explain what fails to detect the classified rings and are not a new
general retraction theorem (Sections~\ref{saut:gr:sec:scope} and~\ref{saut:gr:sec:finite}).
\item The collection's reconstruction and automatic-strongness arguments are reproduced with
attribution, not as novelty (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:detect}).
\item Valuation preservation is an assumption of the Gaussian application; whether every
automorphism of $\Oz[i]$ preserves the Hahn valuation is not resolved
(Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:scope}, Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:questions}).
\item Nor is it shown for arbitrary involutions of $\Oz[i]$; the classification is complete
only in the valuation-compatible Gaussian sector (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:scope}).
\item The normalizer is canonical only relative to the chosen Hahn monomial section; the ring
is not shown to define that section (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:scope}).
\item Real closed fields of transcendence degree $\cc$ are not identified by a finite list of
parameters (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:scope}).
\item ``Geometric'' refers to a multiplicative symmetrization; there is no analytic limit and
no assertion about a surcomplex exponential; $\exp$ in the example is a formal power series
(Sections~\ref{saut:gr:sec:introduction} and~\ref{saut:gr:sec:example}).
\item The formal-inversion theorem of \cite{BKKS} is an imported input; strictly increasing
values alone are not claimed to make an arbitrary geometric operator series meaningful, and
the inverse is not justified by a topological convergence slogan
(Theorem~\ref{saut:gr:thm:inverse}).
\item Strongness is not inferred from continuity, and $\ct$ is not assumed multiplicative on
$K$ (Remark~\ref{saut:gr:rem:circular}).
\item Archimedean real closed fields need not be Dedekind complete or isomorphic to $\R$
(Remark~\ref{saut:gr:rem:arch}).
\item $\Frac(R_F)=\No_F$ uses the boundedness of every set of surreal exponents; it is not
asserted for set-sized Hahn support rings (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:classify}).
\item Exactly $2^{\cc}$ classes is a theorem about the valued Gaussian sector; the constructed
examples stay distinct under arbitrary conjugators, but no upper bound for all pure-field
involutions is claimed (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:count}).
\item Identical finite quotients and integer-coefficient equation solvability do not imply
elementary equivalence; disequations, order constraints and nonordinary coefficients are not
covered (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:finite}).
\item No first-order definability of the rational-cut invariant is asserted
(Remark~\ref{saut:gr:rem:cuts}).
\item The plain-field criterion is not used without the integer-part hypothesis
(Remark~\ref{saut:gr:rem:plain}; see item~29).
\item The $\mathrm H^1$ statement is a Hilbert~90-type reading of the normalizer, not a claim
to originate nonabelian descent; in the class case it is the concrete cocycle identity
(Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:gaussian}).
\item $\mathcal G$ for class maps is shorthand; no class of all class maps is formed, and
counting uses set-indexed representatives (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:inverse}).
\item Phase shears are not claimed new (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:example}).
\item The finite checks do not verify infinite Hahn summability, class-sized constructions,
transcendence bases, cardinalities or all possible automorphisms; the program covers a
homogeneous rank-two test case and is not an algorithm (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:verifier},
Question~\ref{saut:gr:q:effective}, the record's limitations).
\item No Lean verification was performed, the repository's Lean build was not run, and no
repository files were modified (README, Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:verifier}).
\item The research questions are proposed, not published open problems; no named published
open problem is announced as solved, and no unresolved question of \cite{KKS} is claimed
solved (Sections~\ref{saut:gr:sec:questions} and~\ref{saut:gr:sec:position}).
\item The source comparison was targeted, not exhaustive over files, earlier commits,
unintegrated manuscripts or unpublished work (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:position}).
\item The Wikipedia page supplied with the task was used for orientation, not as an input to a
technical claim (\texttt{SOURCES.md}).
\item \emph{(Merge.)} The source's statement that the collection did not supply the
orbit-product normalizer, the classification or the integer-part and cardinality package is
stale (Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:position}); those results are source~02's, and source~03 is
credited as an independent re-derivation.
\item \emph{(Merge.)} The citations of \cite{BKKS} (Theorem~3.11, Proposition~1.45),
including their characteristic-free reading and their application to series on the ordered
exponent set $\Omega$ of the class localization, and of Conrad and the Stacks Project, were
not rechecked for the merge.
\item \emph{(Merge.)} The observation that source~02's descent proof uses no characteristic
zero, the comparison of routes (Remark~\ref{saut:gr:rem:routes}) and
Remark~\ref{saut:gr:rem:kernel} are the merge's.
\item \emph{(Merge.)} Clause (v) of Corollary~\ref{saut:gr:cor:standard} without the
integer-part hypothesis rests on Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:No-criterion} (source~02), and the
example showing that clause (vi) needs it is the merge's; the credits to
\lbl{osq:thm:universal}, \lbl{odg:thm:transfer}, \lbl{odg:thm:finitequotients} and
\lbl{dsn:thm:finite-quotients} are the merge's.
\item \emph{(Merge.)} Of the program's $457$ assertions, $166$ are lexicographic comparisons
that hold by the order; the orbit-product formula is compared with the flow on nine
monomials only; the geometric inverse is tested only where the remainder is nilpotent
(Section~\ref{saut:gr:sec:verifier}).
\item \emph{(Merge.)} No \texttt{saut:gr:} label has a Lean implementation mapping.
\end{enumerate}

\section{A conjugacy criterion for the standard real form}\label{saut:app:conjugacy}

The cofinality obstruction in \cref{saut:thm:inequivalentrealform} has a useful
positive counterpart. Say that an ordered class field has the
\emph{set-cut interpolation property} if for every pair of sets
$\mathcal L,\mathcal R$ with $\mathcal L<\mathcal R$, there exists $x$ with
$\mathcal L<x<\mathcal R$. Empty sides are permitted.

\begin{proposition}\label{saut:prop:conjugacycriterion}
An involution $j$ of $\K$ is conjugate to $c$ if and only if its fixed real
field has the set-cut interpolation property.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The property holds in $\No$ by Conway cuts and is preserved by ordered
isomorphisms. Conversely let $F=\Fix(j)$ have the property. It is a proper
class, since $\K=F(i)$, and it is real closed by
\cref{saut:thm:torsion}. Run the proof of \cref{saut:thm:orderedhomogeneity} between
$F$ and $\No$ rather than within $\No$. At every stage, the only special
existence condition used to extend the partial ordered-field isomorphism
is interpolation of a set-sized cut. It holds on both sides, so
back-and-forth gives $F\simeq\No$. The real-form conjugacy criterion in
Section~\ref{saut:sec:realforms} then gives $j\sim c$.
\end{proof}

This is a structural characterization, not an effective decision procedure
for a given class involution. It singles out precisely the conjugacy class
of the usual surreal real form among all real forms of the pure field.

\emph{Related (batch~32; \mergetag).} The report
\repo{surcomplex/first-kappa-coefficients} constructs, under Global Choice,
one involution of $\K$ for each uncountable cardinal, agreeing with $c$ on
$\C$, whose fixed field is isomorphic to the field of surreals with fewer
terms than that cardinal (\lbl{fkc:sb:thm:involutions}). Those fields have
set-presented gaps, so they fail the set-cut interpolation property, and by
this criterion none of these involutions is conjugate to $c$; that report
records this route (\lbl{fkc:sb:rem:univ}). Two of them attached to
cardinals of different cofinalities are not conjugate, which gives a proper
class of pairwise nonconjugate real forms. They are pure-field involutions.
By Proposition~\ref{saut:fs:prop:conjugacy} none of them lies in the group
$\mathcal G$ of valued automorphisms preserving $\Oz[i]$, whose involutions
Theorem~\ref{saut:fs:thm:main-involutions} classifies; the two families are
complementary.

\section{Provenance and the pinned comparison}\label{saut:app:provenance}

\subsection{Provenance of this report}

This report was built from \emph{one} manuscript (source~01): the archive
\texttt{surcomplex\_automorphisms}, manuscript~07 of batch~20 of the
collection, dated 22 September 2026 and pinned to repository commit
\repoSHA. It was placed in
commit \texttt{5fe7f8d} as a new report rather than merged into
\repo{surreal/exponential-automorphism-rigidity}: it continues that report's
section on $\No(i)$, but with plain field automorphisms instead of
exponential ones, and answers no question that report names. At placement
there was no second source: nothing was merged, and nothing was selected away.
Every result, proof, example, question and limitation of the manuscript is
printed here. \mergetag\ A second source was added in batch~32
(Appendix~\ref{saut:app:source02}), and a third in batch~34
(Appendix~\ref{saut:app:source03}); this subsection and
Appendix~\ref{saut:app:pinned} concern source~01 only.

The delivery consisted of the article source, its PDF and a README; it
contained no code and no data. The source was renamed \texttt{article.tex}
and edited as listed below; the README was rewritten for the collection;
the delivered PDF is not shipped and is replaced by a build of this text.

The editorial changes are these.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item Every label carries the prefix \texttt{saut:}. The source's 79 labels
are kept, unchanged after the prefix, and seven were added
(\texttt{saut:sec:conventions}, \texttt{saut:sec:position},
\texttt{saut:eq:Lchain}, \texttt{saut:sec:nonclaims},
\texttt{saut:app:conjugacy}, \texttt{saut:app:provenance},
\texttt{saut:app:pinned}).
\item The title page gives the report's status and provenance, and the
abstract gains one sentence placing $\AutL(\K)$.
\item Sections~\ref{saut:sec:conventions} and~\ref{saut:sec:position} and
the consolidated non-claims of Section~\ref{saut:sec:nonclaims} are new.
Short pointers were added after Theorem~\ref{saut:thm:decomp}, at the start
of Section~\ref{saut:sec:repo}, and in the first question of
Section~\ref{saut:sec:questions}.
\item Six symbols were renamed so that each has one meaning
(Section~\ref{saut:sec:conventions}): $E_{\rho,\tau}\to M_{\rho,\tau}$,
$E_d\to\Phi_d$, the derivation $D\to\mathcal D$, the Puiseux field
$P\to\mathcal P$, the automorphism $\tau\to\psi$ of the non-density
argument, and cut sides $(L,R)\to(\mathcal L,\mathcal R)$; the source's
$\Aut(\K,L)$ is written $\AutL(\K)$.
\end{enumerate}
The placement edit preserved the source's mathematical statements.
It retained the results reproduced from the rigidity report (its
Lemma~3.1, the faithfulness of Corollary~4.2, the content of
Theorem~5.1 and Corollary~5.2, Theorem~8.4, and Theorem~9.2 with its
kernel clause). They are printed here once, with the
source's proofs and attribution, rather than replaced by pointers, because
they are part of one manuscript's argument; Section~\ref{saut:sec:position}
names each counterpart. The letter $E$ was freed for the exponential,
following the rigidity report, rather than renaming that report's symbol.
The title is the source's; the directory is named
\texttt{surcomplex-field-automorphisms} to say that the automorphisms are
field automorphisms.

The later proof review clarifies rational cuts and strong coefficient action.
It expands the class and support arguments and excludes every derivative
value in $\K$, including infinite values. The finite-observation
non-density proof is extended to all surcomplex field automorphisms.
Current generic Lean coverage is separated from the pinned inspection.
See \repo{REVIEW.md} for the changes and validation.
The added arguments are not attributed to the original delivery.

\item Labels: the source's labels are kept after the prefix \texttt{saut:fs:}, except
